# The research band

The programme map splits the 149 nodes closest to the alleged programme into seven bands, and this file is the research band: 28 nodes, taken era by era from the earliest to the most recent. Each entry gives what the node is, why the board places it here, why it holds the tier it holds, what it has denied, its documented and testimonial claims with their sources, and the links that tie it to the rest of the board.

Names in full, for this file:

- **UAPTF**: Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force

# THROUGH 1969: Estimate through Blue Book

## Robertson Panel

Robertson Panel is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1953; axes C0 A3 D0; 13 connections; in the research band.

In one line: CIA Office of Scientific Intelligence advisory panel, January 1953.

### What it is

The Robertson Panel was a five-member scientific advisory panel convened by the CIA's Office of Scientific Intelligence, meeting 14-18 January 1953, to evaluate whether unidentified flying objects posed a threat to national security. Its proceedings were recorded in a memorandum dated 16 February 1953 from F.C. Durant to the Assistant Director for Scientific Intelligence, known as the Durant Report, with the two-page formal panel report appended as Tab A. It is the founding document of official U.S. UFO information policy.

### Why it is implicated

The Robertson Panel is the moment the U.S. government formally converted a collection problem into a perception-management problem, and it did so in writing. The panel recommended that national security agencies "take immediate steps to strip the Unidentified Flying Objects of the special status they have been given and the aura of mystery they have unfortunately acquired," and Durant's supplement records the panel's concept of an education program with "two major aims: training and 'debunking,'" to be delivered through television, motion pictures and popular articles, with Walt Disney Inc. animated cartoons and broadcaster Arthur Godfrey named as candidate channels. It further recommended that civilian UFO groups "should be watched" for "potentially great influence on mass thinking" and "possible use ... for subversive purposes." The panel's own reasoning turns on hardware: Robertson contrasted the UFO problem with the 1943-1944 CROSSBOW effort, in which "by June 1944 there was material evidence of the existence of 'hardware' obtained from crashed vehicles in Sweden," and concluded that the "absence of any 'hardware'" gave ATIC's problem a "will-of-the-wisp" nature. If hardware existed and was compartmented away from ATIC, the panel's central premise was false and the panel was the instrument by which that falsity was made durable. The CIA then spent 1991 to 1994 telling a FOIA requester it held no records of the panel before finally closing the request.

### The longer account

The Robertson Panel did not originate inside the Air Force. Durant records that "after consideration of the subject of 'unidentified flying objects' at the 4 December meeting of the Intelligence Advisory Committee, the following action was agreed: 'The Director of Central Intelligence will: a. Enlist the services of selected scientists to review and appraise the available evidence in the light of pertinent scientific theories.'" The action was delegated to the Assistant Director for Scientific Intelligence, H. Marshall Chadwell, and the panel was assembled with the cooperation of the Air Technical Intelligence Center. The five members were H.P. Robertson of Caltech, listed in Tab C under field of competency as "Physics, weapons systems"; Luis W. Alvarez of the University of California, "Physics, radar"; Lloyd V. Berkner of Associated Universities, Inc., geophysics; Samuel Goudsmit of Brookhaven National Laboratories, "Atomic structure, statistical problems"; and Thornton Page of the Office of Research Operations, Johns Hopkins University, astronomy and astrophysics. Associate members were J. Allen Hynek of Ohio State and F.C. Durant of Arthur D. Little, Inc. This is a weapons-and-radar panel with a nuclear-physics component, not an atmospheric-phenomena panel.

The panel sat for eight sessions across five days. It viewed the Tremonton, Utah and Great Falls, Montana films, heard Navy Photo Interpretation Laboratory analysts who had spent roughly a thousand professional and sub-professional man-hours plotting the Tremonton frames and concluded the objects were "not reflections because there was no blinking while passing through 60 degrees of arc" and were therefore "self-luminous." The panel rejected that conclusion after watching a short film of seagulls in bright sunlight. It heard Ruppelt on ATIC procedure, Hynek on Battelle's Project STORK, Lt. Col. Frederick Oder on Project TWINKLE, and Dewey Fournet on fifteen months as Pentagon UFO project officer, during which Fournet had eliminated known and probable causes and been left with "extra-terrestrial" as the only remaining explanation in many cases. The panel declined to accept any of Fournet's cases on the grounds that they were "raw, unevaluated reports."

The two-page formal report, Tab A, dated 17 January 1953 and signed by all five members, concluded that the evidence "shows no indication that these phenomena constitute a direct physical threat to national security" and that "there is no residuum of cases which indicates phenomena which are attributable to foreign artifacts capable of hostile acts." The threat it identified was domestic and psychological: continued reporting "does, in these parlous times, result in a threat to the orderly functioning of the protective organs of the body politic," through "the clogging of channels of communication by irrelevant reports, the danger of being led by continued false alarms to ignore real indications of hostile action, and the cultivation of a morbid national psychology in which skillful hostile propaganda could induce hysterical behavior and harmful distrust of duly constituted authority."

Durant's Part II, explicitly labeled "an unofficial supplement to the official Panel Report ... setting forth comments and suggestions of the Panel Members which they believed were inappropriate for inclusion in the formal report", is where the operational content lives. The two-aim education program, the recommended engagement of a mass-psychology consultant (Hadley Cantril of Princeton, author of the study of the Orson Welles panic), the suggestion of an advertising expert, the Jam Handy Co. and Walt Disney Inc. as production channels, the Navy ONR Special Devices Center at Sands Point as an implementing organization, and the estimate that the program would need to run a minimum of one and a half to two years. And the surveillance recommendation regarding the Civilian Flying Saucer Investigators of Los Angeles and the Aerial Phenomena Research Organization.

Two passages deserve the board's attention above the rest. First, under the heading "ARTIFACTS OF EXTRATERRESTRIAL ORIGIN", a standing agenda item, page 10 of the index, Durant records that "none of the members of the Panel were loath to accept that this earth might be visited by extra-terrestrial intelligent beings of some sort, some day," that Goudsmit held such artifacts "are no cause for alarm; rather, they are in the realm of natural phenomena subject to scientific study," and that "this was an attitude in which Dr. Robertson did not concur, as he felt that such artifacts would be of immediate and great concern not only to the U.S. but to all countries." Second, the geographic analysis: the ATIC map of 1952 unexplained sightings "showed clusters in certain strategic areas such as Los Alamos," and "the Panel could find no ready explanation for these clusters."

The document's own release history is evidence. CUFON's Dale Goudie filed FOIA requests in September 1991, November 1991, March 1992 and April 1992. CIA told him the report had been released by the Air Force Office of Public Information on 9 April 1958 and referred him to the Air Force and NARA; NARA told him the Durant Report was in CIA custody. After a search initiated in June 1992, CIA informed him on 6 September 1994 that it was "unable to locate the records requested," and on 28 November 1994 Information and Privacy Coordinator John H. Wright wrote that "we feel it inappropriate to conduct yet another search on your behalf. Therefore, we are unable to assist you further and will not discuss this matter with you further." CUFON documents that multiple non-identical versions of the report, differing in pagination, spacing, typeface and redaction, circulated from CIA over the years, and that no version carries the panel members' signatures.

### What it has denied

The panel's signed conclusion, that there is "no residuum of cases which indicates phenomena which are attributable to foreign artifacts capable of hostile acts" and "no evidence that the phenomena indicates a need for the revision of current scientific concepts", is the template for every subsequent official denial, including AARO's. CIA's own posture is separately documented: it told a FOIA requester for three years that it held no records of a panel it had convened, then declined further discussion in a signed letter of 28 November 1994.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, oversight, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Robertson drafted the panel report on the evening of Friday 16 January and Chadwell reported the next morning that he had shown the rough draft to the Director of Intelligence, USAF, "whose reaction was favorable." Who else saw the draft before it was finalized, and was the conclusion negotiated?
- Berkner was absent until Friday afternoon yet had "reviewed and approved" the draft report earlier than the other members. How?
- What became of the recommended education program? No implementing directive, contract, or budget line for a training-and-debunking program has been produced.
- Was the surveillance recommendation regarding civilian UFO groups acted on, and by which agency? FBI and Air Force OSI files on APRO and NICAP exist but no document ties them to this recommendation.
- Why did CIA hold multiple non-identical, unsigned versions of a single 1953 memorandum, and where is the signed original?
- The panel treated the absence of hardware as dispositive. If recovered materiel existed under compartmented control in January 1953, who at CIA or ATIC knew, and was the panel deliberately kept below that access line?
- The final page of Part II is redacted after the sentence on expected increase in sightings. What was removed?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 100 of 1111, score 62). 15 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 3 official denials recorded against it. Axes C0/A3/D0.

## University of Colorado UFO Project (Condon Committee)

University of Colorado UFO Project (Condon Committee) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1966 to 1969; axes C0 A2 D1; 12 connections; in the research band.

In one line: USAF contract 44620-67-C-0035 (1966-1969).

### What it is

The Condon Committee was the University of Colorado UFO Project, funded by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research from 1966 to 1968 under physicist Edward U. Condon, which produced the report formally titled Scientific Study of Unidentified Flying Objects. Its conclusions, released January 1969, were used by the Air Force as the basis for terminating Project Blue Book on 17 December 1969. It remains the most cited public document on the scientific status of the UFO question.

### Why it is implicated

The Condon Committee is the second and larger instance of the pattern the Robertson Panel established: a nominally independent scientific body whose framing was set before the work began and whose product retired the public investigative apparatus. Robert J. Low's memorandum of 9 August 1966, written to reassure University of Colorado administrators before the contract was signed, states the design explicitly: "The trick would be, I think, to describe the project so that, to the public, it would appear a totally objective study but, to the scientific community, would present the image of a group of non-believers trying their best to be objective but having an almost zero expectation of finding a saucer." The finished report contains an affirmative denial of concealment, "We have no evidence of secrecy concerning UFO reports", offered by a committee that had simultaneously declined to independently evaluate the Air Force's national-security finding, writing "we felt that it was out of our province." A body that will not examine the defense question is not positioned to certify the absence of secrecy about it. The report also recommended against establishing any federal UFO agency, a recommendation that held for fifty-three years until AARO. Buried in Section IV, Chapter 2, the report's own Lakenheath-Bentwaters case assessment concludes "the probability that at least one genuine UFO was involved appears to be fairly high."

### The longer account

The chain of custody runs from the Air Force to itself. In March 1966 an ad hoc committee of the Air Force Scientific Advisory Board chaired by optical physicist Brian O'Brien recommended that the Blue Book program be strengthened, that contracts be negotiated with a few selected universities to provide scientific teams, and that one university or nonprofit coordinate. The Secretary of the Air Force referred the recommendations to the Air Force Office of Scientific Research, which combined recommendations A and C into a single contracting university with subcontracting authority and implemented recommendation B through Air Force Regulation 80-17, reprinted as Appendix B of the eventual report. Harvard, Berkeley, MIT and North Carolina declined. Hynek's Northwestern and McDonald's Arizona were rejected as too committed. Colorado accepted on 6 October 1966. The contract was publicly announced on 7 October 1966 and provided $313,000 for fifteen months, 1 November 1966 to 31 January 1968; total funding later rose above $500,000.

The contract terms are worth reading against what followed. The report states that "the contract provided that the planning, direction and conclusions of the Colorado project were to be conducted wholly independently of the Air Force," that the Air Force was ordered to furnish its records and base support, and that "we were assured that the federal government would withhold no information on the subject." Where sightings involved classified missile launches or classified radar systems, "this fact is merely stated as to do more would involve violation of security on these military subjects." The committee then reports that these reservations "have affected a negligible fraction of the total material." This is an assurance accepted on the word of the contracting party, by a project that had no independent means of verifying either the completeness of what it received or the existence of holdings outside Blue Book.

The Low memorandum predates the contract by two months. Written 9 August 1966 to two university administrators, it reassured them that the study "would be conducted almost entirely by non-believers who, though they couldn't possibly prove a negative result, could and probably would add an impressive body of thick evidence that there is no reality to the observations," before the sentence about the trick. James McDonald learned of it in July 1967 and located a copy in the project's own open files; NICAP severed ties on 30 April 1968; Donald Keyhoe circulated copies; John G. Fuller published "Flying Saucer Fiasco" in Look on 14 May 1968, describing the project as a "$500,000 trick." Condon fired David Saunders and Norman Levine for alleged incompetence; administrative assistant Mary Louise Armstrong resigned; Low resigned in May 1968. Representative J. Edward Roush asked the General Accounting Office to investigate; GAO declined. Condon resigned from the AAAS after Science published on the controversy without his input.

What the committee produced is not a single argument but a document whose front matter contradicts its interior. Condon's six-page Conclusions and Recommendations opens: "Our general conclusion is that nothing has come from the study of UFOs in the past 21 years that has added to scientific knowledge. Careful consideration of the record as it is available to us leads us to conclude that further extensive study of UFOs probably cannot be justified in the expectation that science will be advanced thereby." He recommended against a new federal agency, and on secrecy wrote: "It has been contended that the subject has been shrouded in official secrecy. We conclude otherwise. We have no evidence of secrecy concerning UFO reports. What has been miscalled secrecy has been no more than an intelligent policy of delay in releasing data so that the public does not become confused by premature publication of incomplete studies of reports."

Set that against the adjacent paragraphs. On the defense question Condon wrote: "We felt that it was out of our province to attempt an independent evaluation of this conclusion. We adopted the attitude that, without attempting to assume the defense responsibility which is that of the Air Force, if we came across any evidence whatever that seemed to us to indicate a defense hazard we would call it to the attention of the Air Force at once. We did not find any such evidence. We know of no reason to question the finding of the Air Force." The committee thus deferred to the Air Force on the national-security question and then, three paragraphs later, certified that no secrecy existed. Hynek's later count was that the committee had been unable to explain more than a quarter of the cases it examined. In Case 2 of Section IV, Chapter 2, on the 1956 Lakenheath-Bentwaters radar-visual event, the report states: "although conventional or natural explanations certainly cannot be ruled out, the probability of such seems low in this case and the probability that at least one genuine UFO was involved appears to be fairly high."

The operational effect was immediate and durable. The Air Force had asked the National Academy of Sciences, before the report was finished, to independently assess its scope, methodology and findings; a panel chaired by Yale astronomer Gerald M. Clemence studied it for six weeks and endorsed it, concluding that "no high priority in UFO investigations is warranted by data of the past two decades." On 17 December 1969 the Secretary of the Air Force announced the termination of Project Blue Book, citing the Colorado report, the NAS review, past studies and Air Force experience. The Air Force fact sheet distributed from Wright-Patterson in January 1985 records the closing tally, 12,618 sightings reported 1947 to 1969, of which 701 remain "Unidentified", and appends a categorical denial: "There are not now nor ever have been, any extraterrestrial visitors or equipment on Wright-Patterson Air Force Base." That sentence, on a public affairs fact sheet from the base that housed ATIC and let the Battelle contract, is a dated, attributable claim about the specific installation most often named in recovery accounts. It is evidence about the denier.

### What it has denied

Condon's Section I contains one of the two most consequential official denials in the record: "We have no evidence of secrecy concerning UFO reports." It was issued by a body that had explicitly declined to evaluate the defense question, that acknowledged its access to classified missile and radar material was limited by the contracting agency, and whose project coordinator had described the public framing as a trick two months before the contract was signed. The Air Force's January 1985 Wright-Patterson fact sheet supplies the companion denial about physical custody.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, oversight, research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- The report states the Air Force assured the project that no information would be withheld. No document memorializing that assurance, and no accounting of what classified missile or radar material was in fact withheld, has been produced.
- Was the Colorado project given access to anything outside the Blue Book file series, ATIC's Battelle-era holdings, foreign technology division files, or any compartmented program?
- Condon stated in a January 1967 lecture that he thought the government should not study UFOs because the subject was nonsense, "but I'm not supposed to reach that conclusion for another year." Who, if anyone, in the Air Force knew of that statement at the time?
- The GAO declined Rep. Roush's request to investigate the project. On what stated grounds, and does a written GAO declination exist?
- Fifty-nine case studies were published with their locations altered "for legal reasons." The key mapping location-to-case has never been released.
- Low resigned in May 1968; no further contemporaneous account of the August 1966 memo's audience or purpose has surfaced.
- Why did the Air Force commission an NAS review of the report before the report was finished?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 140 of 1111, score 53). 13 connections, 3 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A2/D1.

## Project MKULTRA

Project MKULTRA is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1953-04-13 to 1964; axes C0 A3 D2; 9 connections; in the research band.

In one line: CIA umbrella project for research and development of materials to control human behavior; authorized by DCI Dulles 13 April 1953, records destroyed January 1973.

### What it is

The CIA's umbrella project for research and development of materials to control human behavior, authorized by Director of Central Intelligence Allen W. Dulles in April 1953 and run out of the Technical Services Division through 149 numbered subprojects at universities, foundations, hospitals and prisons. Its normal procedures for project approval, funding and accounting were waived. Its records were destroyed in January 1973 on a verbal order, and seven boxes of finance folders survived only because they had been misfiled to the Retired Records Center in 1970.

### Why it is implicated

It is the documented American precedent for the whole of this board's argument about compartmented programmes and their records: a waived-procedure compartment, an Inspector General finding that the practice was to keep no records of the planning and approval of test programmes, a destruction order that two Senate committees could not defeat, and a single Freedom of Information Act request that did. The board's own MKULTRA hearing node argued from this programme for two months without carrying it.

### The longer account

The argument for putting MKULTRA on a board about a legacy recovery programme is not that the two are the same subject. It is that MKULTRA is the case where every mechanism this board alleges was later proven on the government's own paper, and can therefore be used as a calibration rather than as an analogy. The Inspector General's survey of 26 July 1963 states the purpose in the Agency's own words and states, in the same document, that normal procedures for project approval, funding and accounting were waived and that present practice was to maintain no records of the planning and approval of test programs. That is a compartment designed to leave no trail, described by its own auditor. Ten years later the trail that did exist was destroyed: Technical Services Division personnel burned the files in January 1973 on Sidney Gottlieb's verbal order, itself carrying out a verbal order from DCI Helms, and both men later testified to it. The Rockefeller Commission and the Church Committee investigated the programme in 1975 without the finance file, because the 1975 searches covered the records of every branch thought likely to hold MKULTRA papers and not the Budget and Fiscal Section's retired holdings. What broke it open was administrative accident plus a statute. One branch had sent its finance folders to the Retired Records Center in 1970 in a departure from normal procedure the Agency could not later explain, and in 1977 an employee working John Marks's Freedom of Information Act request found them. The Senate's own opening statement puts the lesson plainly: one individual, through a Freedom of Information request, accomplished what two US Senate committees could not. Everything on this board about custody, about destruction orders and about what survives them has a documented instance here.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether any of the 149 subproject files identifies its researchers: the released Subproject 43 file excises every occurrence of the researcher's name and university.
- What the 33 subprojects unrelated to behavioural work in the recovered boxes actually cover.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 155 of 1111, score 51). 9 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A3/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** The Church Committee text reprinted in the hearing print records the destruction and the chain behind it: 'In January 1973, MKULTRA records were destroyed by Technical Services Division personnel acting on the verbal orders of Dr. Sidney Gottlieb, Chief of TSD. Dr. Gottlieb has testified, and former Director Helms has confirmed, that in ordering the records destroyed, Dr. Gottlieb was carrying out the verbal order of then DCI Helms.' Source: Project MKULTRA, The CIA's Program of Research in Behavioral Modification, joint hearing, 3 August 1977, Appendix B, https://www.intelligence.senate.gov/sites/default/files/hearings/95mkultra.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Inspector General's 1963 survey states the programme's purpose: 'The MKULTRA activity is concerned with the research and development of chemical, biological and radiological materials capable of employment in clandestine operations to control human behavior.' It records that 'MKULTRA was authorized by the then Director of Central Intelligence, Mr. Allen W. Dulles, in 1953' and that 'Normal procedures for project approval, funding, and accounting were waived.' Source: Report of Inspection of MKULTRA, memorandum for the Director of Central Intelligence, 26 July 1963, National Security Archive John Marks Collection Box 8, https://nsarchive.gwu.edu/sites/default/files/documents/1963-07-26%20JM%20Box%208%20F2%20MKUltra-IG_Report-ocr.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The recovered material covers 149 numbered subprojects plus two boxes of audit reports and financial statements from cut-out funding mechanisms and 33 further subprojects unrelated to behavioural work. Turner told the committee the new identities were 'the names of 185 nongovernment researchers and assistants' and 'names of 80 institutions', comprising 44 colleges or universities, 15 research foundations or chemical or pharmaceutical companies, 12 hospitals or clinics and 3 penal institutions. The hearing's own opening statement gives a different figure, 'Eighty-six universities or institutions were involved.' - **Tier 1 documented.** The same survey records the programme's own record-keeping practice: 'Present practice is to maintain no records of the planning and approval of test programs.' The Director of Central Intelligence quoted that sentence back to the Senate in 1977 as part of the reason so little was known even before the files were destroyed. Source: Report of Inspection of MKULTRA, 26 July 1963, p. 14, quoted in the prepared statement of Admiral Stansfield Turner, Project MKULTRA joint hearing, 3 August 1977, https://www.intelligence.senate.gov/sites/default/files/hearings/95mkultra.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The surviving records are finance folders and they survived by accident. DCI Stansfield Turner reported 'our recent discovery of seven boxes of documents related to Project MKULTRA', found at the Retired Records Center because the Budget and Fiscal Section had sent them there in 1970 in what the statement calls a departure from normal procedure whose reason 'is not known'. As a result 'the material escaped retrieval and destruction in 1973 ... as well as discovery in 1975'. ## Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14

Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14 is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1955; axes C1 A2 D2; 6 connections; in the research band.

In one line: 'Analysis of Reports of Unidentified Aerial Objects', Project No. 10073, 5 May 1955; prepared by Battelle Memorial Institute.

### What it is

Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14, formally titled 'Analysis of Reports of Unidentified Aerial Objects,' dated 5 May 1955, is the largest statistical study of UFO reports the U.S. government has ever produced. The analysis was performed under contract by Battelle Memorial Institute of Columbus, Ohio, under a project designation reported as Project STORK, using 3,201 cases with an information cut-off of the end of 1952. It was released publicly on October 25, 1955 through Department of Defense Office of Public Information Release No. 1053-55.

### Why it is implicated

The gap between what the study found and what the government said it found is the cleanest documented instance of deliberate framing in the entire record, and it is verifiable from two documents that were published together. The press release and Secretary of the Air Force Donald Quarles's accompanying statement told the public that the unknowns amounted to three per cent, a figure drawn from 131 sightings in the first four months of 1955, not from the 3,201-case study sample the report actually analyzed. The report body shows unknowns running far higher, and highest precisely where witness reliability was rated Excellent. The release also declined to name Battelle, describing the analysis only as the work of a private scientific group under the supervision of the Air Technical Intelligence Center at Dayton, Ohio. The full 316-page report was then not distributed, on stated grounds of printing cost, until a private citizen reprinted it. Every element of a concealment operation is present and dated: a suppressed dataset, a substituted statistic, an unnamed contractor, and a restricted distribution.

### The longer account

Start with what the report says about itself. Its own Summary states that the special study started in 1953, that to provide the study group with a complete set of files the information cut-off date was established as of the end of 1952, and that in these charts, 3201 cases have been used. It describes a three-part analytic method: a systematic attempt to find distinguishing characteristics in the data, a study of any trend or pattern, and an attempt to determine the probability that any of the UNKNOWNS represented observations of technological developments not known to this country. On the first of these it records a genuinely load-bearing finding: 'The first step in the analysis of the data revealed the existence of certain apparent similarities between cases of objects definitely identified and those not identified. Statistical methods of testing when applied indicated a low probability that these apparent similarities were significant.' Read plainly, Battelle tested whether the unknowns looked like the knowns and found that the resemblance did not hold up. That is the opposite of the conclusion the public was handed. The report then walks it back through re-evaluation, concluding that the probability the unknowns represented unknown technology was very small.

Now the release. Department of Defense Office of Public Information Release No. 1053-55, dated October 25, 1955 and headlined 'AIR FORCE RELEASES STUDY ON UNIDENTIFIED AERIAL OBJECTS', opens with the flat assertion that no evidence of the existence of the popularly-termed flying saucers was found. It attributes the work to a private scientific group under the supervision of the Air Technical Intelligence Center at Dayton, Ohio, and never says the word Battelle. It then executes the substitution: since the instigation of the investigation more than seven years ago, methods and procedures have been so refined that of the 131 sightings reported during the first four months of 1955 only three per cent were listed as unknown. Secretary Quarles then attaches his name to that number: 'On the basis of this study we believe that no objects such as those popularly described as flying saucers have overflown the United States. I feel certain that even the unknown three per cent could have been explained as conventional phenomena or illusions if more complete observational data had been available.' The phrase on the basis of this study is doing enormous work. The study is the 3,201-case Battelle analysis. The three per cent is from a 131-case sample the study did not analyze, collected three years after the study's own cut-off date. The two are welded together in a single sentence and a Cabinet-level official signs it.

The attached Summary makes the substitution visible in its own numbers. For cases received before the end of 1952 it gives Unknown at 9 per cent; for the more recent tranche it gives Unknown at 3 per cent. Neither figure is the rate for the 3,201 cases whose charts and tables make up the report.

The body tells a different story, which is why the body was hard to get. Leon Davidson, a chemical engineer who had worked at Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory and who reprinted the report photographically beginning in 1956, wrote in his publisher's analysis that the key to the answer is contained in Fig. 8 (orig. p. 24) and Tables A1, A2, and A3 (orig. pp. 107 and 108); that Fig. 8 shows Unknown sightings constitute 33.3 per cent of all the object sightings for which the reliability of the sighting is considered Excellent; and that Tables A1 through A3 agree, with the percentage of Unknown sightings not less than about 20 per cent even when sightings of lesser reliability are included. The distribution runs the wrong way for the official framing: the better the witness, the more likely the object stayed unidentified.

Distribution was the second layer of the concealment. Davidson reproduces a letter from Air Force Secretary Donald A. Quarles dated July 5, 1956 stating that it has been estimated that the cost of printing enough copies for distribution to the public through such outlets as libraries and academic institutions would be between $10 and $15 per copy. The 316-page full report was therefore never distributed. The non-availability became a matter of congressional record at the House Subcommittee on Government Information under Rep. John E. Moss on November 15, 1956. Davidson's privately financed edition, reproduced photographically page by page with the main text intact, is why the document exists in public hands at all.

One further anomaly sits in the photographic reproduction. Davidson points to original page 295, where the codes for Final Identification carry typewritten strikeovers: the category originally called Rockets and Missiles in the early analytic work was changed to read Insufficient Information, and the category originally called Electromagnetic Phenomenon was changed to read Unknown. If that reading of the page is correct, then the report's residual category was not an empty bin for the unexplained. It was a renamed bin for a hypothesis.

Battelle is the connective tissue. The same institute later formed UT-Battelle, the operator of Oak Ridge National Laboratory, which per contemporary allegations received AARO's 2024 material analysis work. Battelle is also one of the entities Rep. Burlison named in his May 2026 MITRE interrogatories, alongside Lockheed Martin, Northrop Grumman, Raytheon, Boeing, General Dynamics, SAIC, Leidos, The Aerospace Corporation, Booz Allen Hamilton, BAE Systems and Bigelow Aerospace. The organization that wrote the government's founding public statistical assessment of the phenomenon has never left the file.

### What it has denied

The official denial is the October 25, 1955 release itself and Secretary Quarles's attached statement, which is exactly why it is evidence rather than argument. It is dated, numbered (Release No. 1053-55), signed by a named Cabinet-level official, and its central statistical assertion can be checked against the document it purports to summarize. The Air Force position that no objects such as those popularly described as flying saucers have overflown the United States was published simultaneously with, and contradicted by, the report's own reliability-stratified unknown rates.

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- The widely circulated figure of 21.5% unknowns among the 3,201 cases could not be verified against a legible primary table in the sources retrieved; the verifiable primary-adjacent figures are 33.3% for Excellent-reliability sightings and not less than about 20% overall, per Davidson's reading of Tables A1 through A3
- Whether the significance tests Battelle ran are recoverable from the surviving full tables, and at what confidence level the knowns and unknowns were found to differ
- Who inside ATIC or the Office of Public Information decided to substitute the 1955 131-case three per cent figure for the study's own unknown rate, and whether that decision is documented
- Who authorized the strikeover changes to the Final Identification codes on original page 295, and when
- Whether the Battelle contract file, deliverables and the intermediate Project STORK status reports survive, and in whose custody
- Whether Battelle's institutional records on this work fall within the scope of Rep. Burlison's 2026 FFRDC and contractor records campaign

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 147 of 1111, score 52). 6 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A2/D2.

## Ansel E. Talbert, three-part gravity series, New York Herald Tribune, 20-22 November 1955

Ansel E. Talbert, three-part gravity series, New York Herald Tribune, 20-22 November 1955 is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1955-11-20 to 1955-11-22; axes C0 A0 D3; 6 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Three front-page articles by Ansel E. Talbert, Military and Aviation Editor, New York Herald Tribune, 20, 21 and 22 November 1955.

### What it is

Three consecutive front-page articles by Ansel E. Talbert, Military and Aviation Editor of the New York Herald Tribune, published on 20, 21 and 22 November 1955, all three page images opened and read in full.

### Why it is implicated

It is the contemporaneous press record behind every later claim that American aerospace executives said on the record in the 1950s that gravity could be controlled, and it is the document that dates and corrects those claims. It names the firms, quotes Lawrence D. Bell and George S. Trimble jr. directly, reports the Chapel Hill gravity institute under an earlier name, and mentions Thomas Townsend Brown nowhere in its three parts.

### The longer account

This series is the load-bearing document under a whole branch of the electrogravitics literature, and reading it moves four things at once. It dates the reporting to November 1955 rather than 1953. It gives George Trimble his actual office, vice-president in charge of advanced design planning of Martin Aircraft, rather than chief executive, and shows that his atom-bomb comparison is about the time the bomb took and not about the money it cost. It names the Sperry Gyroscope Division of Sperry-Rand among the firms with scientists on the gravity problem, which is the documented attachment for a node the 30 August pass left deliberately unattached. And it reports the University of North Carolina trustees approving a gravity institute earlier that month under the name Institute of Pure Physics, with Gordon Gray's approval, Agnew H. Bahnson jr. as fundraiser and the directorship offered to Bryce S. DeWitt, which is the Institute of Field Physics before it had its final name. What it does not contain is the figure the electrogravitics claim usually rests on: Thomas Townsend Brown is named in none of the three articles, and neither Winterhaven nor any Navy or Air Force electrogravitics contract appears in them. That absence is carried here as a claim and deliberately not as an edge.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the Herald Tribune ran any follow-up or correction to the series.
- What the 'Institute of Pure Physics' name change to Institute of Field Physics turned on, which the series does not say.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 547 of 1111, score 29). 6 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D3.

Source: Talbert, New York Herald Tribune, 20 November 1955 (caption and continuation p. 36) and 22 November 1955 (continuation p. 10). https://archive.org/download/talbert1ny/talbert1ny_djvu.txt and https://archive.org/download/talbert3ny/talbert3ny_djvu.txt

# 1970-1999: Post-Blue Book

## COMETA - Comité d'Études Approfondies

COMETA - Comité d'Études Approfondies is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1996 to 2006; axes C0 A1 D3; 11 connections; in the research band.

In one line: French 1901-law association of retired general officers and IHEDN auditors, 1996-c.2006.

### What it is

Retired French generals, admirals and IHEDN auditors put the defence question in writing and named the extraterrestrial hypothesis

### Why it is implicated

COMETA is the document that has no American equivalent. A committee of retired French flag officers - an air force general in the chair, a vice-admiral, a weapons engineer general, a chief of police from the Interior Ministry, a research director from ONERA - spent close to three years reviewing UAP case material, wrote ninety pages under the title 'UFOs and Defense: What Should We Prepare For?', concluded that the extraterrestrial hypothesis could not be excluded and was the most credible of the available explanations, and put their names on it. It was not commissioned by the French state, and its authors are careful about that. But it was written by people the French state had trained to think about national defence, using material from the French state's own UAP office, and it reads as a defence staff study rather than a polemic.

### The longer account

COMETA is the document that has no American equivalent. A committee of retired French flag officers - an air force general in the chair, a vice-admiral, a weapons engineer general, a chief of police from the Interior Ministry, a research director from ONERA - spent close to three years reviewing UAP case material, wrote ninety pages under the title 'UFOs and Defense: What Should We Prepare For?', concluded that the extraterrestrial hypothesis could not be excluded and was the most credible of the available explanations, and put their names on it. It was not commissioned by the French state, and its authors are careful about that. But it was written by people the French state had trained to think about national defence, using material from the French state's own UAP office, and it reads as a defence staff study rather than a polemic.

The committee's institutional centre of gravity is the Institut des Hautes Études de Défense Nationale. Almost every named member is an IHEDN auditor, identified by promotion number: Michel Algrin, doctor of political science and attorney-at-law (AA 35); Pierre Bescond, Weapons Engineer General, 2nd Section (AA 48); Denis Blancher, Chief of Police, Police Nationale, Ministry of the Interior; Jean Dunglas, honorary engineer general (AR 48); Bruno Le Moine, Air Force General, 2nd Section (AA 41); Françoise Lepine of the Fondation pour les Études de Défense (AA 33); Christian Marchal, research director at ONERA; Marc Merlo, Admiral, 2nd Section (AA 35); and Alain Orszag, Weapons Engineer General, 2nd Section. General Denis Letty, Air Force, 2nd Section (AA 35), chaired it. The report carries a foreword by Professor André Lebeau, former chairman of CNES, and one by General Bernard Norlain, former director of IHEDN, whom Letty visited at IHEDN in March 1995 to explain the project. Letty thanks both, saying that without them 'COMETA would not have been born'.

The report's own account of its lineage matters for this board because it closes a loop back to GEIPAN. COMETA's introduction states that in 1976 an IHEDN committee chaired by General Blanchard of the Gendarmerie Nationale opened the unidentified flying objects file with the objective of making proposals for organising research and data collection, that 'the goal was achieved, because the recommendations of this committee were followed in the creation of' GEPAN, and that twenty years later it seemed useful to take stock again. In other words the same institution produced both the French state's UAP office and, two decades on, the committee that reviewed its output.

The evidentiary base is the French official record. Contributors included Jean-Jacques Velasco, then head of SEPRA at CNES; François Louange, chief executive of the image-analysis firm Fleximage; the pilots Jean-Charles Duboc, Jean-Pierre Fartek and René Giraud; Edmond Campagnac, former technical director of Air France at Antananarivo; Squadron Commander Michel Perrier of the Gendarmerie Nationale; an official of the DGAC; and Air Force General Joseph Domange. Letty specifically thanks the commander of the Air Force Air Operations Command Center for its participation in the investigation into Air France flight AF 3532 of 28 January 1994.

On substance, COMETA reproduces GEPAN/SEPRA's four-category scheme and then states the number that the whole document turns on: 'Category D UAPs represent 4 to 5% of the cases and are called UAP Ds.' Category D means unidentified despite the abundance and quality of the data - not unidentified for want of it. The report's position is that a residue of that size, often attested by consistent testimony and by visual sightings coupled with radar, can no longer be filed as no action: 'if there had only been ten or so UAP D [sightings], this ambiguous file could just have been classified as "no action," but we are no longer at that point and are far beyond that. Thus we are forced to seek plausible explanations.' It records around one hundred GEPAN/SEPRA investigations, several of which 'demonstrated, in the end, the physical presence of a phenomenon the nature and origin of which remain unknown', singling out Trans-en-Provence (8 January 1981) and 'Amaranth' (21 October 1982), documented in GEPAN technical memoranda 16 and 17. On aeronautical cases it records twelve French cases, only three or four of them category D; the first, in 1951, involving Vampire aircraft near Orange; and AF 3532 on 28 January 1994 as the first French case of a visual sighting correlated with radar over fifty seconds. It also draws on Dominique Weinstein's enumeration of 489 well-documented aeronautical UAP D cases worldwide since 1942, most from official sources.

COMETA is structured in three parts - facts and testimonies, the extent of current knowledge, and UFOs and defence - plus a conclusion and seven appendices, among them 'Radar Detection in France', 'Life in the Universe', 'Colonization of Space' and 'The Roswell Affair - Disinformation'. Its recommendations are set out in the introduction: give civilian and military pilots enough information to adopt adapted conduct when faced with these phenomena, and inform the public and decision-makers more generally; develop the actions of SEPRA and promote supplemental scientific monitoring and research; and consider the strategic, political and religious consequences of a possible confirmation of the extraterrestrial hypothesis, 'the bizarre connotation of which it is advisable to eliminate here and now'. Letty's own framing, in the foreword, is the sentence most often quoted from the document and is verbatim: 'The accumulation of well-documented sightings made by credible witnesses forces us to consider from now on all of the hypotheses regarding the origin of unidentified flying objects, or UFOs, and the extraterrestrial hypothesis, in particular.'

COMETA the association was registered under the French law of 1901 on 24 February 1999, having operated informally since 1996, and went dormant around 2006. The report appeared in July 1999 in a special issue of VSD, was reissued by Éditions du Rocher in 2003, and was not widely circulated in its original form because of copyright restrictions. According to Robert Roussel, citing Olivier Schrameck, Prime Minister Lionel Jospin read and annotated it 'to mark his interest'. There was no formal government response beyond that. GEIPAN made an archive of the report public in 2007.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 120 of 1111, score 56). 11 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A1/D3.

### Its strongest connections

- COMETA - Comité d'Études Approfondies runs out to **Project Blue Book** (program, board tier A), a research link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: adopts Hynek's Blue Book-era classification. COMETA states that the strangest category D cases 'are generally labeled CE3s (close encounters of the third kind) according to the classification proposed by Professor A. Hynek, an astronomer and consultant to the USAF, within the context of the Blue Book Project'. A committee of retired French flag officers adopting Blue Book's own consultant's taxonomy is the inheritance this edge records. Sources: COMETA, 'UFOs and Defense: What Should We Prepare For?', 1999, chapter 7.2, https://ia800403.us.archive.org/17/items/pdfy-NRIQie2ooVehep7K/The%20Cometa%20Report%20%5BUFO%27s%20And%20Defense%20-%20What%20Should%20We%20Prepare%20For%5D.pdf, dated 1999-07-01.
- COMETA - Comité d'Études Approfondies runs out to **Roswell Incident** (event, board tier A), a research link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: dedicated appendix on Roswell and disinformation. COMETA's Appendix 5 is titled 'The Roswell Affair - Disinformation'. A committee of retired French flag officers treating Roswell as a subject requiring an appendix in a defence study is itself part of what this edge records; the report's own framing places it under disinformation rather than under evidence. Sources: COMETA, 'UFOs and Defense: What Should We Prepare For?', 1999, Appendix 5, https://ia800403.us.archive.org/17/items/pdfy-NRIQie2ooVehep7K/The%20Cometa%20Report%20%5BUFO%27s%20And%20Defense%20-%20What%20Should%20We%20Prepare%20For%5D.pdf, dated 1999-07-01.
- COMETA - Comité d'Études Approfondies comes in from **GEIPAN - Groupe d'Études et d'Informations sur les Phénomènes Aérospatiaux Non identifiés** (institution, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: shared IHEDN lineage; SEPRA head contributed. ## SETKA-AN / SETKA-MO (later Galaktika, then Gorizont) - Soviet state anomalous phenomena programme

SETKA-AN / SETKA-MO (later Galaktika, then Gorizont) - Soviet state anomalous phenomena programme is a program on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1978-10-18 to 1991; axes C1 A2 D2; 11 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Twin USSR Academy of Sciences and Ministry of Defence programmes, 1978-1990/91.

### What it is

Twin USSR Academy of Sciences and Ministry of Defence programmes, 1978-1990. The Soviet Union turned its entire army into a standing UAP sensor network for thirteen years, and then published

### Why it is implicated

SETKA is the largest state UAP data-collection effort ever run, and almost nothing about it looks like the American arrangement. It was created by a bureaucratic decision minuted at the Kremlin, funded out of nobody's budget, executed by fewer than a dozen people, mandated in writing to every Soviet serviceman, and - the part that matters here - written up afterwards by the two men who ran it and published in the Academy of Sciences' own journal.

### The longer account

SETKA is the largest state UAP data-collection effort ever run, and almost nothing about it looks like the American arrangement. It was created by a bureaucratic decision minuted at the Kremlin, funded out of nobody's budget, executed by fewer than a dozen people, mandated in writing to every Soviet serviceman, and - the part that matters here - written up afterwards by the two men who ran it and published in the Academy of Sciences' own journal.

The trigger was the Petrozavodsk phenomenon of 20 September 1977, a large-scale luminous event seen across the north-west USSR and into Finland by ambulance crews, militia, dockers, military personnel and airport staff, and reported in Izvestia three days later under the headline 'The Unidentified natural phenomenon'. Local authorities asked the Presidium of the Academy of Sciences to explain it; letters arrived from northern European governments asking whether the effects came from military-technical experiments and endangered the environment. Under that pressure the President of the Academy of Sciences, Academician Anatoly Aleksandrov, wrote to L. Smirnov, Vice-President of the government and Chairman of the Military-Industrial Commission, asking that a complex research programme on anomalous phenomena be started with Ministry of Defence and war-industry involvement. The MIC Scientific and Technical Council met in the Kremlin in October 1977 under Lieutenant-General B. A. Kijasov. Members noted that reports of extraordinary phenomena also reached Ministry of Defence organisations from servicemen, and had sometimes coincided with equipment failures.

The meeting recommended, and the MIC under the Council of Ministers adopted, the inclusion of two topics in the 1978 state plan of defence research: 'Setka MO' (Ministry of Defence) - 'Research of paranormal atmospheric and space phenomena and their influence on the operation of military technical equipment and personnel' - and 'Setka AS' (Academy of Sciences) - 'Research of a physical nature and mechanisms of paranormal atmospheric and space phenomena'. The programme ran without interruption for thirteen years, to 1990, and was re-approved in the five-year defence research plans of 1981 and 1986. Platov and Sokolov note that the October 1977 STC meeting 'was the first and last measure with a so high official level of the participants engaged in the problem'.

The execution was tiny. The military branch was led from one of the central military research institutes in the Moscow region, with its chief V. Balashov - an expert on radiation and other damaging factors on military equipment - as scientific head, and four to five specialists doing the collection, processing and analysis. Around fifteen further Ministry of Defence and war-industry organisations participated voluntarily and very limitedly. The academic branch was led by Academician V. Migulin, director of IZMIRAN, which was designated head institute; the working group there, also four to five people, was headed by Yu. Platov. Budget financing covered salaries and travel, drawn from the executing organisations' own budgets, with no special allocation - 'the most inexpensive one, if not the cheapest, among the scientific works on defensive subjects'.

The instrument that made it work was a document authorised by the Chief of the General Staff in January 1980 and distributed to the troops as a General Staff Directive. It designated the central military research institute as the collection, processing and analysis centre for all UAP information obtained by the military; named the research institutes responsible in all five arms; set the procedure by which the centre could obtain identification data, above all launch data for military aerospace systems; and fixed the rules for documenting detections and the reporting order by urgency. The authors' own summary is the sentence worth having verbatim: the directive 'for 13 years put the army into the on-duty regime of observation of the paranormal phenomena in the places of the army dislocation, i.e. practically on the whole territory of the USSR'. Every serviceman became, as they put it, 'without knowing it' a potential participant, obliged to report any inexplicable phenomenon in writing.

The work was classified for three stated reasons: it sat inside the classified defence research plan; a military-technical origin for the effects was thought likely at the outset; and there was a possible military application of properties such as absence of radar contrast and high manoeuvrability. The word 'UFO' was not used in official documents, which said 'anomalous phenomenon' instead - partly because it fitted the observations better, partly to keep clear of popular agitation about aliens arriving on flying saucers.

The results are what a large sensor network with a small analysis cell produces. Over thirteen years about three thousand messages arrived, describing more than 400 distinct events after mass-observation reports were consolidated. More than 90% were resolved as effects accompanying rocket and space launches - sunlight scattered off gas-and-dust clouds from rocket exhaust, visible over hundreds of kilometres in twilight geometry - or as high-altitude balloons, which accounted for 10-12% of messages. The Petrozavodsk event itself was attributed to the launch of Kosmos 955 from Plesetsk. Notably, reports did not come in from units at or near test ranges, where such effects were familiar. The 1984-87 series of flight emergencies at the Borisoglebsk air unit in Voronezh region, where unknown objects appeared on radar and were seen by pilots, the authors leave explicitly unidentified.

And then the finding that makes the programme worth a node rather than a footnote. Across thirteen years of compulsory reporting spanning roughly a sixth of the Earth's land surface, the programme received no report of a landing, no report of contact with occupants, and no report of an abduction. Platov and Sokolov put the conclusion as a fork: either the USSR was closed to alien visitation for at least thirteen years, or the extraterrestrial-origin hypothesis is inconsistent. They also dismiss the 1990s wave of claims about 'KGB secret files' and 'classified capture of UFO' as absurd. Whatever one makes of the conclusions, the contrast with the American record is the point: the Soviet state ran the collection openly enough inside its own system to mandate it army-wide, and its coordinators published the account under their own names in Vestnik Rossiiskoi Akademii Nauk in 2000.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 203 of 1111, score 45). 11 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A2/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** 'During the work on the program (for 13 years) about three thousand of messages reporting about extraordinary phenomena have been obtained ... on the whole, more than 400 events were registered and identified as extraordinary or paranormal.' Over 90% were resolved as rocket-launch effects or high-altitude balloons, the balloon class accounting for 10-12% of messages. The Petrozavodsk phenomenon itself was attributed to 'the launch of an artificial Earth satellite "Kosmos 955" from the space range in Plesetsk'. Reports 'did not practically come' from units at or near test ranges, where such effects were familiar. Source: Platov and Sokolov (2000) - http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** 'The budget financing of the research program included the salary of the experts occupied in these activities and the costs of business trips. All the money was coming from the budget of organisations - executors and there was no special financing of the research.' The programme was 'the most inexpensive one, if not the cheapest, among the scientific works on defensive subjects'. Source: Platov and Sokolov (2000) - http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** 'Thus, in 1978 in the USSR the state program of studying the "UFO" phenomenon started, and proceeded without interruption for 13 years down to 1990.' Five-year defence research plans including the anomalous-phenomena work were approved again in 1981 and 1986. Source: Platov and Sokolov (2000) - http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** A document authorised by the Chief of the General Staff in January 1980 reached the troops as a General Staff Directive; it designated the central research institute as the collection, processing and analysis centre, named responsible institutes in all five arms, set the order for obtaining launch data needed for identification, and fixed rules for documenting and reporting detections by urgency. Source: Platov and Sokolov (2000) - http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** Academy of Sciences President Anatoly Aleksandrov wrote to L. Smirnov, Vice-President of the government and Chairman of the Military-Industrial Commission, requesting urgent consideration of a complex research programme on anomalous phenomena involving Ministry of Defence and war-industry organisations. - **Tier 1 documented.** Across the whole programme there was no message about a UFO landing, no message about contact with UFO occupants, and no message about abductions. The authors state the conclusion as a fork: 'either the territory of the USSR was, due to any reasons, closed for alien visitations during, at least, 13 years or that the hypothesis of an extraterrestrial origin of "UFO" is inconsistent.' Source: Platov and Sokolov (2000) - http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** The MIC Scientific and Technical Council met 'in October, 1977 in Kremlin under the presidency of general - lieutenant B.A.Kijasov'; members noted that reports of extraordinary phenomena also reached Ministry of Defence organisations from servicemen and that 'during such events sometimes were marked the cases of violation in the operation of the means of maintenance of the army forces'. Source: Platov and Sokolov (2000) - http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Petrozavodsk phenomenon of 20 September 1977 was reported in Izvestia on 23 September 1977 under the title 'The Unidentified natural phenomenon', describing a 'huge "star" with impulsive beams of light coming to the ground' that hung over the town 'as a huge "jellyfish"'. Source: Yu. V. Platov and B. A. Sokolov, translated from Vestnik Rossiiskoi Akademii Nauk, Vol. 70, No. 6, 2000, pp. 507-515 - http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** The military branch was led from 'one of the central military research institutes in the Moscow region', with its chief V. Balashov as scientific head and a working group of four to five people; roughly fifteen further Ministry of Defence and war-industry organisations participated voluntarily. The academic branch was headed by Academician V. Migulin, director of IZMIRAN, which was 'determined as the head institute in this field of activity', with a working group of four to five headed by Yu. Platov. Source: Platov and Sokolov (2000) - http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** The research was classified for three stated reasons: it formally entered the classified defence plan; a military-technical origin for the effects was thought likely at the outset; and there was 'a capability of a military use of some probable properties of the UFO ... absence of a radar contrast, high manoeuvrability, etc.' In official documents 'the use of an abbreviation "UFO" was not accepted, instead of it the term "paranormal phenomenon" was used.' Source: Platov and Sokolov (2000) - http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html

Source: Platov and Sokolov (2000) - http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** Two topics were entered in the 1978 defence research plan, verbatim: '"Setka MO" (Ministry of Defence) - "Research of paranormal atmospheric and space phenomena and their influence on the operation of military technical equipment and personnel"; "Setka AS" (Academy of Sciences) - "Research of a physical nature and mechanisms of paranormal atmospheric and space phenomena".' Sokolov's own 'History of State-directed UFO Research in the USSR': the programmes 'formally belonged to activities pertaining defense-related subjects; initial assumption that there is a high probability of military-technical origin of the observed strange phenomena; and possibilities that in case of successful completion of the raised tasks some of the UFO characteristics could be used for military purposes.' ### Its strongest connections

- SETKA-AN / SETKA-MO (later Galaktika, then Gorizont) - Soviet state anomalous phenomena programme runs out to **United States Department of Defense (secondary designation: Department of War)** (institution, board tier A), a behavioral link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: opposite reporting posture: mandated versus stigmatised. A January 1980 Directive of the Soviet General Staff obliged every serviceman to report any inexplicable phenomenon in writing and routed the results to a single analysis centre, putting the army 'into the on-duty regime of observation' for thirteen years. The contrast with the US chain of command over the same period, where reporting carried career risk and no service-wide collection mandate existed, is the comparison this node exists to make. The Soviet side of the comparison is documented by the programme's own coordinators; no institutional contact between the two is claimed. Sources: Stonehill & Mantle - Setka: A Secret Soviet UFO Research Program, https://web.archive.org/web/20101119234133/http://aforteanosla.com.ar/Colaboraciones/internacional/Mantle,%20Philip/Stonehill,%20Paul%20and%20Mantle,%20Philip.%20Setka%20an%20article.pdf, dated 2010-11-19; Project Condign Volume 3 (UK MoD assessment of FSU activity), https://archive.org/download/condign-vol-2-1-258/Condign_Vol_3_1-48_djvu.txt, dated 2006-05-15; http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html, http://www.debunker.com/historical/HistoryRussianUFOlogy.html

- SETKA-AN / SETKA-MO (later Galaktika, then Gorizont) - Soviet state anomalous phenomena programme comes in from **Operação Prato (Operation Saucer)** (program, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: COMPARISON: two military UAP field-collection programmes that ended in publication rather than burial. COMPARISON EDGE, no contact between the Brazilian and Soviet programmes is claimed or evidenced, and none is likely. The pairing is structural: Operação Prato was a small dedicated Air Force and SNI field detachment run under a military dictatorship that closed with an official negative finding and whose files later went to a civil national archive; SETKA was a thirteen-year standing collection tasking laid on the whole Soviet armed forces, written up afterwards by the men who ran it and published in the Academy of Sciences' own journal. Both are counter-examples to the US pattern in the same way and by opposite routes, one through an archive law, one through its own operators publishing. ## CEFAA / SEFAA - Chilean anomalous aerial phenomena office, Dirección General de Aeronáutica Civil

CEFAA / SEFAA - Chilean anomalous aerial phenomena office, Dirección General de Aeronáutica Civil is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1997-10-03; axes C1 A1 D3; 9 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Chilean civil aviation UAP office, 1997-present (renamed SEFAA 2021).

### What it is

A civil aviation regulator that has treated UAP as a flight-safety problem, on the record, since 1997

### Why it is implicated

Chile's UAP office exists because pilots kept reporting things and a civil aviation authority decided that was an aviation-safety matter rather than an embarrassment. That framing - not disclosure, not exobiology, but the safety of air operations in 32 million square kilometres of airspace - is what has kept it alive for nearly thirty years and what makes it the South American counterweight on this board.

### The longer account

Chile's UAP office exists because pilots kept reporting things and a civil aviation authority decided that was an aviation-safety matter rather than an embarrassment. That framing - not disclosure, not exobiology, but the safety of air operations in 32 million square kilometres of airspace - is what has kept it alive for nearly thirty years and what makes it the South American counterweight on this board.

The prehistory runs back to 1968. Anomalous aerial phenomena were first reported officially in Chile at the old Oficina Meteorológica de Chile in the Quinta Normal aeronautical complex in Santiago, where the director, Aviation Colonel Sergio Bravo Flores, began collecting reports and led the Comisión Chilena para Estudios de Fenómenos Espaciales No Identificados, formed with the support of the Sociedad Científica de Chile. Bravo's first act, on 9 July 1968, was to circulate 43 aeronautical installations from Arica to the Chilean Antarctic asking for reports. The commission - a meteorologist from Línea Aérea Nacional, an engineer, a physician, a doctor of physical and mathematical sciences, a Chilean Air Force lieutenant seconded to the aeronautics directorate, and several engineers - stated its object as establishing 'with all seriousness the true causes' of the matter. In seven years it gathered around a dozen reports, some of which were forwarded to the University of Colorado, where the panel later known as the Condon Committee was at work. It wound up in 1975 when Bravo retired.

The modern office was created twenty-two years later. A wave of sightings reported over Arica in the last days of March and first days of April 1997, together with the expressed interest of the former Commander-in-Chief of the Chilean Air Force, General del Aire Ramón Vega Hidalgo, moved the Director General of Civil Aeronautics, Aviation General Gonzalo Miranda Aguirre, to create the Comité de Estudios de Fenómenos Aéreos Anómalos on 3 October 1997, by Resolución Exenta N° 01599. The resolution sited the committee at the Escuela Técnica Aeronáutica in Quinta Normal, Santiago, and on 14 October 1997 the school's director, Colonel Enzo Di Nocera García, set out its organisation and operating basis and named its members: himself as head, air traffic controller Gustavo Rodríguez Navarro, and the heads of the air traffic and meteorology areas as consultants, with ETA specialists and external organisations convened as needed. In practice Rodríguez Navarro carried the office's work through its early years.

The institutional history is not a straight line, and the dossier is stronger for saying so. Di Nocera led the committee until 1998, when Air Brigade General Ricardo Bermúdez Sanhueza took over the ETA and with it CEFAA, running it to February 2002. Air Brigade General Hernán Henríquez Cobaise took the directorship in March 2002 and held it to 31 March 2008, and the DGAC's own account describes the period from March 2002 to March 2008 as 'una etapa de latencia' - a dormancy. On 15 December 2009, by Resolución N° 02/0/01937, Director General José Huepe Pérez ordered the committee to operate from 1 January 2010 reporting directly to the Secretaría General, detaching it from the ETA and putting its members on permanent, exclusively investigative postings. Bermúdez returned to the directorship in 2009 and served to 2016; Hugo Camus Palacios succeeded him. On 18 October 2021, Resolución Exenta N° 04/3/0113/1385 repealed the 1997 resolution and renamed the office the Sección de Estudios de Fenómenos Aéreos Anómalos, moving it under the DGAC's Subdepartamento Comunicacional. It is now based at Av. Pedro Aguirre Cerda 5100, Cerrillos, Santiago.

Its remit is defined by airspace, not by belief. SEFAA's mission is to support the safety of air operations in the national territory and in the airspace of DGAC responsibility, which extends across 32 million square kilometres from Arica in the north, between 120° and 131° west, to the South Pole. Its principal source is pilot reports - military, commercial, private, sport and institutional - supplemented by public reports, and it explicitly reserves the right to select which material is investigable. Investigations are grounded in the scientific method and depend heavily on audiovisual records. A digital case database was created in 2018; by September 2021 the office had received 1,675 cases in total, with 362 arriving in 2020 alone, nearly a 300% jump on prior years, and 300 more by September 2021. Since 2020 investigated and resolved cases have been published on the office's website, with reports made available to the public quarterly, and resolved-case pages remain live by year through 2026. The permanent staff is small - in 2018 the committee comprised its director and two investigators - but it draws on an external research committee of astronomers, geographers, physicists, psychologists, a nuclear chemist, aerospace medicine specialists, air traffic controllers, a meteorologist, aeronautical investigators, inspector pilots, aeronautical engineers, image analysts and armed forces representatives. On its 24th anniversary the DGAC described CEFAA as 'uno de los organismos públicos más antiguos del continente' and as leading a transparency model comparable, allowing for the difference in human and budgetary resources, to a European government body.

Its most visible case is the Chilean Navy footage of 11 November 2014, in which an Airbus Cougar AS-532 helicopter crew recorded roughly ten minutes of infrared video of an object off the coast west of Santiago. CEFAA investigated for over two years and released the footage with an official report in January 2017; Bermúdez, who had directed the investigation, summarised the outcome as knowing what it was not rather than what it was. The case is also the clearest documented instance of European-South American technical cooperation on UAP: photo and video analysts in France, including François Louange, were brought in through GEIPAN, and GEIPAN's analysts proposed a mundane explanation - a medium-haul aircraft venting - that CEFAA rejected. Independent analysts subsequently matched the timestamps and geometry to scheduled flights. The disagreement is the point: two state UAP offices on different continents worked the same tape, reached different conclusions, and both published.

That visibility is now institutional. Canada's Office of the Chief Science Advisor, designing a national UAP reporting system in 2025, described SEFAA as one of only three national models worth examining alongside the United States and France, and recommended that Canada's lead organisation establish partnerships with AARO and NASA, GEIPAN and SEFAA.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 88 of 1111, score 67). 9 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A1/D3.
