The two offices reached different conclusions - GEIPAN's analysts proposed a medium-haul aircraft venting, CEFAA rejected it - and both published. It is the clearest documented case of state UAP offices on two continents working the same tape. Sources: https://www.huffpost.com/entry/groundbreaking-ufo-video-just-released-from-chilean_b_586d37bce4b014e7c72ee56b, https://www.huffpost.com/entry/groundbreaking-ufo-video-just-released-from-chilean_b_586d37bce4b014e7c72ee56b; https://www.newsweek.com/chilean-navy-id-mysterious-aircraft-caught-video-539786, https://www.newsweek.com/chilean-navy-id-mysterious-aircraft-caught-video-539786
- CEFAA / SEFAA - Chilean anomalous aerial phenomena office, Dirección General de Aeronáutica Civil comes in from **Peruvian Air Force anomalous aerial phenomena office (OIFAA / DIFAA)** (institution, board tier C), a institutional link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: COMPARISON: a military-hosted office against a civil-aviation-hosted one, in the same region. COMPARISON EDGE, no joint work is claimed. Andina names Chile among the regional peers DIFAA's reopening brought Peru level with, and reports Peru's stated posture on cooperation as reserved: it considered itself able to analyse the phenomena independently and would appeal to exchange with neighbours only exceptionally, to compare data. Structurally the two are opposites: DIFAA sits inside the Fuerza Aérea del Perú under DINAE, while CEFAA/SEFAA sits inside Chile's civil aviation authority, the DGAC, and frames the subject as an aviation-safety matter. Tier 2, the structural contrast is analytic, the Peruvian facts are Andina-sourced and the Chilean facts sit on the cefaa and dgac-chile nodes. Sources: Agencia Peruana de Noticias Andina (Editora Perú), 'Fenómenos aéreos anómalos en Perú podrán reportarse a la FAP', 18 October 2013, https://andina.pe/agencia/noticia-fenomenos-aereos-anomalos-peru-podran-reportarse-a-fap-478982.aspx; Andina (Agencia Peruana de Noticias), 'Fenomenos aereos anomalos en Peru podran reportarse a la FAP', 18 October 2013, https://andina.pe/agencia/noticia-fenomenos-aereos-anomalos-peru-podran-reportarse-a-fap-478982.aspx, dated 2013-10-18.
- CEFAA / SEFAA - Chilean anomalous aerial phenomena office, Dirección General de Aeronáutica Civil runs out to **Chilean Navy helicopter FLIR case, November 2014 (released January 2017)** (event, board tier C), a investigative link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: analysed the Navy FLIR case for two years then released it. The Chilean office published the raw sensor product without an explanation, three years before the US DoD authorised release of the Navy videos. Sources: New York Post, https://nypost.com/2017/01/09/chilean-navy-admits-it-cant-explain-ufo/, dated 2017-01-09.

# 2000-2016: AAWSAP / AATIP

## Office of the Director of National Intelligence

Office of the Director of National Intelligence is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2005-04-22; axes C1 A3 D3; 18 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Head of the 18-element Intelligence Community; co-signatory of the UAP reporting regime.

### What it is

The Office of the Director of National Intelligence is the coordinating headquarters of the US Intelligence Community, created by the Intelligence Reform and Terrorism Prevention Act of 2004 and headed by the Director of National Intelligence. Since 2021 it has been the statutory co-owner of the government's UAP reporting machinery: it published the June 2021 'Preliminary Assessment: Unidentified Aerial Phenomena,' it is the office with which the Secretary of Defense must coordinate in establishing and running AARO, and it co-issues the annual consolidated UAP reports. Its Inspector General is also the channel through which UAP whistleblower complaints reach the congressional intelligence committees.

### Why it is implicated

ODNI sits on both sides of the ledger, and that is precisely what makes it useful. It is the office that publicly certified that most reported UAP 'probably do represent physical objects,' that eleven documented pilot near-misses exist, and, critically, that it could not confirm that any of the collected reports were attributable to classified US government or industry developmental programs. An intelligence headquarters that cannot deconflict a UAP dataset against its own black programs is either not being told, or is telling us it is not being told. At the same time, ODNI is the institutional counterparty for every DoD SAP that shares compartmented capabilities with the IC, and AARO is lodged inside the DoD office designated as ODNI's primary interface. Its Inspector General found David Grusch's complaint alleging concealed retrieval and reverse-engineering programs 'credible and urgent' and forwarded it to the DNI and both intelligence committees, after which nothing publicly followed. Even the FOIA-released version of its own 2021 assessment is redacted under (b)(1) and (b)(3), including the identity of the official it names as accountable.

### The longer account

ODNI's formal entry into the UAP file came through an appropriations rider rather than through the intelligence community's own initiative. Senate Report 116-233, accompanying the Intelligence Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2021, directed the DNI, in consultation with the Secretary of Defense, to submit an intelligence assessment of the threat posed by unidentified aerial phenomena and of the progress the Department of Defense Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force had made in understanding it. The resulting nine-page unclassified 'Preliminary Assessment: Unidentified Aerial Phenomena,' dated 25 June 2021, is the single most-cited government document on the subject, and it repays close reading of the FOIA copy rather than the sanitised public one.

The drafting roster alone is analytically valuable. The report was 'drafted by the UAPTF and the ODNI National Intelligence Manager for Aviation, with input from USD(I&S), DIA, FBI, NRO, NGA, NSA, Air Force, Army, Navy, Marine Corps, DARPA, FAA, NASA, NOAA, ODNI/NIM-Emerging and Disruptive Technology, ODNI/National Counterintelligence and Security Center, and ODNI/National Intelligence Council.' That is a list of every organisation with an equity in the file as of 2021, and it is a map of where records exist.

The substantive findings are hedged but not empty. 'Most of the UAP reported probably do represent physical objects given that a majority of UAP registered across multiple sensors, to include radar, infrared, electro-optical, weapon seekers, and visual observation.' The UAPTF 'has 11 reports of documented instances in which pilots reported near misses with a UAP.' The dataset is limited primarily to US government reporting of incidents from November 2004 to March 2021. The report proposes five explanatory bins, airborne clutter, natural atmospheric phenomena, USG or US industry developmental programs, foreign adversary systems, and a catchall 'other', and then, on the third bin, delivers the sentence a program hypothesis must build on: 'Some UAP observations could be attributable to classified USG aerospace programs or systems under development by commercial aerospace firms. We were unable to confirm, however, that these systems accounted for any of the UAP reports we collected.' On foreign adversaries: 'We currently lack data to determine any UAP are part of a foreign collection program or indicative of a major technological advancement by a potential adversary.' On collection: 'The UAPTF had no MASINT reporting on the events considered in this dataset', which stands in direct tension with the later Immaculate Constellation allegation that a parent USAP holds high-quality IMINT and MASINT of UAP.

The redaction pattern is itself evidence. The FOIA version, approved for release on 24 March 2022 under case DF-2021-00275, still withholds material under (b)(1), properly classified national defence information, and (b)(3), statutory exemption. The sentence identifying the accountable official reads, in the released copy, 'The UAPTF Director, is the official accountable for ensuring the timely collection and consolidation of data on UAP,' with the name removed. Passages describing unusual flight characteristics, GEOINT and SIGINT collection, and specific incidents are blank. The unclassified public product is therefore a curated fraction of what Congress received, and the delta is where the argument lives.

ODNI's second role is structural. Under 50 U.S.C. 3373 the Secretary of Defense must act 'in coordination with the Director of National Intelligence' in establishing AARO, and the reporting mechanism must be established 'in consultation with the Director of National Intelligence.' AARO is housed inside OUSD(I&S). And DoDD 5205.07, effective 12 September 2024 and approved by Deputy Secretary of Defense Kathleen Hicks, designates USD(I&S) as the office that 'serves as the primary interface for the Office of the Director of National Intelligence (ODNI) and members of the Intelligence Community (IC), including sharing with the DoD compartmented capabilities and information intended for inclusion and subsequent apportionment.' In other words, the same office that hosts the body Congress created to find unreported UAP programs is the office through which IC compartmented capabilities are moved into and out of DoD special access programs. Whether that is an oversight design flaw or the point is the question the board exists to press.

The third role is the whistleblower channel. In 2022, while at the National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency, David Grusch filed a Presidential Policy Directive 19 urgent-concern complaint with the Intelligence Community Inspector General. Thomas Monheim, IC Inspector General from April 2020 to January 2025, found the complaint credible and urgent, and a summary went to the Director of National Intelligence and to the House and Senate intelligence committees. Grusch then testified publicly to House Oversight in July 2023. No ODNI product has since publicly addressed the substance of the referral.

Finally, ODNI has been the co-publisher of record for the successor reporting. The Fiscal Year 2024 Consolidated Annual Report on Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena, released 14 November 2024 the day after the House Oversight hearing, reported 757 UAP reports received, 485 of which occurred within the reporting period and 272 of which occurred in 2021-2022 but were reported late; 118 cases resolved to prosaic objects; 21 unresolved. That report is a DoD/AARO product carried on the ODNI website, and under this board's sourcing rules it is a primary record of what the government said, not of what is true.

What the record does not show is any ODNI statement about retrievals, materials, or reverse-engineering. There is no ODNI acknowledgement of a legacy program, and no public ODNI response to the Grusch referral.

### What it has denied

ODNI has never denied that a legacy retrieval or reverse-engineering program exists; it has denied confirmation. Its 2021 assessment states it was 'unable to confirm' that US classified aerospace programs accounted for any collected UAP report, and that it 'currently lacks data' to attribute UAP to a foreign collection program. The characteristic ODNI posture is epistemic hedging plus redaction, not categorical denial, which is a materially different artifact from the flat DoD denials on this board.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, concealment, disclosure, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Who is the UAPTF Director redacted under (b)(6) in the FOIA copy of the 2021 assessment?
- What does the classified annex to the 2021 preliminary assessment contain, and who at ODNI has seen it?
- What action, if any, did ODNI or the DNI take on the ICIG's credible-and-urgent referral of the Grusch complaint?
- Has ODNI or any IC element transferred records to NARA Record Group 615, and what did they withhold?
- Which ODNI element holds equity in any UAP-related compartmented capability shared with DoD SAPs through the USD(I&S) interface?
- Why could the IC not deconflict its own UAP dataset against classified US aerospace programs, was the deconfliction attempted and refused, or never possible at the UAPTF's access level?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 124 of 1111, score 55). 18 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A3/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** DoD Directive 5205.07, effective 12 September 2024 and approved by Deputy Secretary of Defense Kathleen H. Hicks, designates USD(I&S) as the office that 'serves as the primary interface for the Office of the Director of National Intelligence (ODNI) and members of the Intelligence Community (IC), including sharing with the DoD compartmented capabilities and information intended for inclusion and subsequent apportionment', the same office in which AARO is housed. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf

We were unable to confirm, however, that these systems accounted for any of the UAP reports we collected.' It carries 18 connections in total on the board.

## GEIPAN - Groupe d'Études et d'Informations sur les Phénomènes Aérospatiaux Non identifiés

GEIPAN - Groupe d'Études et d'Informations sur les Phénomènes Aérospatiaux Non identifiés is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2005; axes C1 A1 D3; 10 connections; in the research band.

In one line: CNES unit, Toulouse; the world's only permanent civilian state UAP office with a public case database.

### What it is

France has run a state UAP office inside its space agency continuously since 1977 and publishes the case files

### Why it is implicated

GEIPAN is the answer to the question the US posture keeps deflecting: what does it actually look like when a government decides that unidentified aerospace phenomena are a normal object of public administration. The answer is a two- or three-person office in a space agency, a volunteer investigator corps, a steering committee of police, gendarmes, air force, meteorologists and scientists, a published four-tier classification, and a searchable public archive of every closed case. It has run without interruption since 1977 under three names.

### The longer account

GEIPAN is the answer to the question the US posture keeps deflecting: what does it actually look like when a government decides that unidentified aerospace phenomena are a normal object of public administration. The answer is a two- or three-person office in a space agency, a volunteer investigator corps, a steering committee of police, gendarmes, air force, meteorologists and scientists, a published four-tier classification, and a searchable public archive of every closed case. It has run without interruption since 1977 under three names.

CNES states plainly on its own site that it has studied unidentified aerospace phenomena since 1977, the year GEPAN - the Groupe d'Étude des Phénomènes Aérospatiaux Non identifiés - was created. Its origin was not a scandal or a leak but a recommendation: a 1976 Institut des Hautes Études de Défense Nationale committee, chaired by General Blanchard of the Gendarmerie Nationale, opened the UFO file with the explicit objective of making proposals for organising research and data collection, and GEPAN was created on the back of that committee's recommendations. That lineage is stated in the COMETA report's own introduction. Claude Poher directed GEPAN at its inception. In 1988 GEPAN was replaced by SEPRA, the Service d'Études des Phénomènes de Rentrées Atmosphériques, which absorbed the UAP function into a unit whose principal job was routine monitoring of orbital debris and re-entry risk objects. At the end of 2001 the CNES Direction Générale commissioned an audit to decide the activity's future. The 2005 outcome was restructuring rather than closure: GEIPAN replaced SEPRA, and the audit carried what CNES calls 'une recommandation importante d'informer le public en toute transparence' - an important recommendation to inform the public in complete transparency. That recommendation is embedded in the acronym; the added 'I' stands for Information. It was implemented starting in 2007 with the online publication of GEIPAN's archives and case files.

The structure is worth stating precisely because it is the operational counter-model. GEIPAN sits under the deputy directorate of the Toulouse Space Centre. It is steered by a comité de pilotage (COPEIPAN) chaired by a recognised figure from the aerospace world and composed of representatives of the country's civil and military authorities - Gendarmerie nationale, civil aviation, meteorology, the Air and Space Force, scientific research - plus CNES. That committee's job is to analyse GEIPAN's results and make recommendations to CNES on its direction. Beneath it sits a collège d'experts of roughly thirty multidisciplinary volunteers who take the hardest cases, and a corps of volunteer field investigators across France who are mandated case by case and work to the methods set out in GEIPAN's investigator guide. GEIPAN holds standing conventions with the Gendarmerie, the Air Force, civil aviation, the Navy and Météo France for rapid access to hard data, and works with CNRS and the CEA. The permanent staff, per GEIPAN's own history page, is two full-time CNES agents - the head and an assistant - supported by external contractors for case analysis, statistics, anonymisation and archiving. Canada's Office of the Chief Science Advisor, surveying the office in 2025, counted three full-time employees and over 20 trained volunteer investigators. Funding comes out of the general public-service subsidy CNES receives; there is no separate UAP line item. Frederic Courtade has headed GEIPAN since January 2024.

The classification is what makes the archive usable. Each case is scored on two axes - consistency (C), the product of the quantity and reliability of data, and residual strangeness (E) after comparison against known-phenomenon hypotheses - then filed as A (perfectly identified), B (probably identified), C (unidentified for lack of data) or D (unidentified after investigation). Cases in C and D can be reopened if new information arrives. As of the site statistics dated 11 August 2026, 3,381 published classified cases break down as 950 class A (28.1%), 1,310 class B (38.7%), 1,015 class C (30.0%) and 106 class D (3.1%). Canada's report, working from the 2025 figures, recorded 3,188 cases with 3.2% unidentified after investigation and 31.4% unexplained for insufficient data. The residual is small, it is stable, and it is public.

GEIPAN is also unusually explicit about what it is not. Its FAQ states that it is not an independent structure but a technical department of CNES; that it carries no national-security, space-surveillance or alert mission; and that, 'contrary to popular belief', it is not a research group on UFOs, extraterrestrial life or other paranormal phenomena. It uses only recognised scientific knowledge, 'without speculation or unverified hypotheses'. That disclaimer is not evasion - it is the price of the archive, and it is precisely the trade the US system has refused to make.

The substantive work is on the record. In June 1978 GEPAN presented its scientific council with a five-volume, roughly 500-page report on the first semester of 1978; a contemporaneous English-language summary of that report, held in the NSA's public UFO document collection, records that eleven high-credibility, high-strangeness cases were studied in detail, that only one proved to have a conventional explanation, that in ten of the eleven the conclusion was that witnesses had seen a material phenomenon explicable as neither a natural phenomenon nor a human device, and that the report concluded there was behind the phenomenon a 'flying machine. . . whose modes of sustenance and propulsion are beyond our knowledge'. The same summary judges that the care taken with distances, angles and psychological factors 'makes the bulk of the Condon Report seem very poor by comparison'. GEPAN's most cited physical-trace investigation, the Trans-en-Provence landing of 8 January 1981, was published as Note Technique n° 16, 'Analyse d'une trace', issued by CNES in Toulouse on 1 March 1983 and still hosted on the GEIPAN site.

The office's standing outside France is the argument in compressed form. When Canada's Chief Science Advisor set out to design a national UAP reporting system in 2025, GEIPAN was the model it described in the most detail, the example it explicitly told Canada to consider when establishing a dedicated service, and one of only four international bodies - alongside AARO, NASA and Chile's SEFAA - that it recommended Canada partner with.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 86 of 1111, score 68). 10 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A1/D3.

## Unidentified Aerial Phenomena in the UK Air Defence Region (DIS Scientific & Technical Memorandum 55/2/00)

Unidentified Aerial Phenomena in the UK Air Defence Region (DIS Scientific & Technical Memorandum 55/2/00) is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2000 to 2006-05-15; axes C1 A2 D3; 9 connections; in the research band.

In one line: THREE-volume UK Defence Intelligence Staff report (not four); the separately-released standalone Executive Summary is what has been miscounted as a fourth part. Volume 3 preface is dated February 2000. Released 15 May 2006 under FOI to David Clarke and Gary Anthony.

### What it is

Project Condign was a classified UK Ministry of Defence study of unidentified aerial phenomena carried out for the Defence Intelligence Staff between 1996 and 2000, resulting in a three-volume, roughly 460-page report titled 'Unidentified Aerial Phenomena in the UK Air Defence Region.' It drew on approximately 10,000 UK sighting reports held by DI55, the Defence Intelligence branch responsible for UFO investigations inside the Directorate of Scientific and Technical Intelligence. Classified 'Secret UK Eyes Only,' it was released in May 2006 after a Freedom of Information Act request by researchers David Clarke and Gary Anthony.

### Why it is implicated

Condign is the clearest documented case of a NATO partner's intelligence service running a multi-year, compartmented UAP program while telling the public and Parliament almost nothing. Its debunking conclusion sits alongside an explicit recommendation that the plasma phenomenon it invoked be examined for 'novel military applications,' with the implications already briefed to MoD technology managers, a technology-exploitation tasking hiding inside a dismissal document. Most importantly, the sections the MoD fought hardest to keep censored for two decades turned out not to concern aliens at all but rumoured US black programs (Aurora/Astra and the TR-3A 'Black Manta'), and the MoD's stated justification in February 2026 was that release 'could still provide adversaries with an insight into the developments of US/UK aircraft technology.' That is a government stating on the record that UAP files are classified in order to protect black aerospace programs, and doing so on behalf of a foreign partner. Condign also contains a precise, dated, attributable negative that any retrieval hypothesis has to answer: that no artefacts of unknown origin had ever been handed to UK authorities.

### The longer account

Project Condign is the UK's answer to the question of what an intelligence service actually does with a national UFO file when it thinks nobody is watching. The MoD had, since the 1950s, maintained a nominal UFO desk that handled public correspondence. Behind it sat DI55, a section of the Directorate of Scientific and Technical Intelligence within the Defence Intelligence Staff, which quietly collected sighting reports for defence-intelligence purposes. In 1996 DI55 commissioned defence scientist Ron Haddow, whom, according to David Clarke, DI55 regarded as their subject expert, to write a comprehensive assessment. Haddow was given access to the retained MoD holdings, worked for four years, and delivered in 2000 a three-volume study running to roughly 460 pages. It was classified Secret UK Eyes Only and, by Clarke's account, shown to only a handful of people.

The report's public reputation is as a debunking. That is a fair reading of its headline conclusions: 'There is no evidence that any UAP, seen in the UKADR, are incursions by air-objects of any intelligent (extra-terrestrial or foreign) origin, or that they represent any hostile intent,' and 'No artefacts of unknown or unexplained origin have been reported or handed to the UK authorities, despite thousands of UAP reports. There are no SIGINT, ELINT or radiation measurements and little useful video or still IMINT.' Those are unusually specific negatives, and they are the strongest single piece of counter-evidence a British-side retrieval narrative has to overcome. An honest board records them as such.

But the report is not only a debunking, and this is where it becomes load-bearing for the program argument. Its residual-explanation hypothesis is 'Buoyant Plasma Formation', an atmospheric plasma phenomenon it proposes as the cause of black-triangle reports and of close-encounter perceptual effects, via electromagnetic fields that could 'adversely affect a vehicle or person.' Having proposed it, the report does not stop at explanation. It recommends further research into novel military applications of the phenomenon and records that 'the implications have already been briefed to the relevant MoD technology managers.' It also notes that scientists in the former Soviet Union had 'identified the close connection between the UFO Phenomena and Plasma technologies' and were 'pursuing related techniques for potential military purposes.' In other words: a classified UAP study concluding there is nothing to see also concluded that there was something worth weaponising, and routed it to acquisition people. That is the structural signature of an exploitation channel, and it is on the record in an executive summary the MoD itself released.

The second thread is the redactions. When Clarke received the report in 2006, handed to him in MoD Main Building by Linda Unwin, then the penultimate UFO desk officer, a post abolished in 2009, sections of volume 2 stamped NATO Secret were withheld. The MoD held that line for two decades through repeated FOI requests, invoking FOIA exemptions 24 (national security) and 27 (international relations), and as recently as February 2026 refused release on the grounds that disclosure 'could still provide adversaries with an insight into the developments of US/UK aircraft technology, compromising the military capabilities of US partners and our own Air Force assets.' Four months later the identical material was released in full to an anonymous requester. What it contained were two speculative paragraphs on the rumoured Aurora/Astra programme and on the TR-3A 'Black Manta,' with a figure annotating the latter as having a 60-foot wingspan and 42-foot length, described as 'covert survivable in weather strike/recce,' in two variants, one 'seen with sausage-shaped contrail (or smoke?)' and one 'probably/possibly with pulsating motor.' Also withheld had been head-on silhouettes of the F-117 and B-2 with the observation that their fuselage shapes 'can be reported respectively as saucer or arc/boomerang shaped under certain silhouette and lighting conditions,' and the note that 'these aircraft types are unfamiliar as they are rarely spotted in UK airspace.'

The analytic yield is twofold. First, the UK's defence intelligence establishment in the 1990s privately believed the Americans were flying triangular and diamond-shaped stealth platforms through UK airspace, with or without permission, and used that belief as an explanatory bin for domestic UAP reports, the same move AARO makes today. Second, the twenty-year classification of that material demonstrates the mechanism the board is trying to characterise: UAP secrecy sustained not because of what UAP are, but because black-program equities, including a partner nation's, attach to the same file. Clarke's own judgment on the 2006 release was that it shed no new light on the phenomenon but did show DIS had been running a far larger investigation than it had ever admitted.

What the record does not show is any Condign statement about materials, retrievals, or reverse-engineering, it explicitly denies artefacts. It says nothing about US programs beyond the Aurora and TR-3 speculation. And because the study was never peer reviewed, the plasma hypothesis has been challenged as one that would not have survived independent scrutiny.

### What it has denied

The report itself is the denial artifact: a dated, signed, government-authored statement that no UAP in UK airspace are of intelligent extra-terrestrial or foreign origin and that no unknown artefacts were ever handed to UK authorities. The MoD separately maintained for two decades, most recently in February 2026, that specific redacted sections could not be released without compromising US/UK aircraft technology, a national-security justification it abandoned four months later by releasing the same material in full.

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Who inside DI55 tasked Haddow, and under what standing requirement was DI55 collecting UAP reports in the first place?
- Which 'relevant MoD technology managers' received the plasma briefing, and did any research programme follow?
- Did any US agency review, coordinate on, or object to portions of the report before or after the 2006 release, and is there a US-side copy?
- What is in volume 3, and does the surviving original three-volume copy the MoD rediscovered (per Clarke's 2023 'Lost and Found' post) differ from the 2006 release?
- Does the Condign statement that no artefacts were ever handed to UK authorities extend to materials held by UK contractors or transferred to US custody, or only to direct handovers to the MoD?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 97 of 1111, score 64). 9 connections, 3 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A2/D3.

## Project Condign Volume 3 - Potential for Exploitation of UAP-Associated Effects / UAP Work in Other Countries

Project Condign Volume 3 - Potential for Exploitation of UAP-Associated Effects / UAP Work in Other Countries is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2000 to 2006-05-15; axes C1 A2 D3; 7 connections; in the research band.

In one line: UK SECRET UK EYES ONLY volume; the international-liaison chapter.

### What it is

Volume 3 of the UK Ministry of Defence Defence Intelligence Staff study "Unidentified Aerial Phenomena in the UK Air Defence Region", issued as Scientific & Technical Memorandum 55/2/00 by DI55 within DI(ST) from the Old War Office Building, Whitehall, and titled on its own contents page "MISCELLANEOUS RELATED STUDIES". Its preface is dated February 2000 and states plainly: "This is the third volume of a three-part report on the information held in DI55 on the subject of UAP." Every page is header- and footer-stamped UK SECRET / UK EYES ONLY, and the volume was released to the public on 15 May 2006 under the Freedom of Information Act 2000 with redactions under sections 26, 27 and 40.

### Why it is implicated

Volume 3 is the volume where a NATO ally's defence intelligence staff wrote down, in a classified assessment, the two things the United States has spent seventy years insisting do not exist: a chapter titled "POTENTIAL FOR EXPLOITATION OF UAP-ASSOCIATED EFFECTS" and a chapter titled "UAP WORK IN OTHER COUNTRIES". Chapter 3 opens by stating that one of the reasons for analysing UAP reports at all "was that of identifying any phenomena which could be robustly generated and provide a military advantage" - and the fourteen lines that follow that sentence are blacked out under Section 26 (Defence), as is the entire body of the page listing what DI55 called "three possible related potential applications". Chapter 4 then catalogues what other states were doing, naming Russian institutions, scientists, cosmonauts, laboratories and a research centre by name, and confirming in the Executive Summary that "DI55 does not have access to any other Government's classified reports, as there is no formal intelligence exchange on this topic." That last sentence is the load-bearing one for the board: the UK's own defence intelligence organisation, an ally inside the closest intelligence-sharing arrangement on earth, records that it was locked out of everyone else's UAP files - including, by necessary implication, Washington's. And the countries DI55 named as having staff active on the subject were redacted not under Defence but under Section 27, International Relations.

### The longer account

The Condign report is usually reduced to a single soundbite - the plasma hypothesis - and Volume 3 is the volume that shows why that reduction is a mistake. Volume 1 carried the statistical analysis and the public-facing conclusions. Volume 2 carried the working papers. Volume 3 carried the material the study's author explicitly said "could not be placed in the RESTRICTED Executive Summary". Its own preface concedes the reason it is stamped SECRET is narrow and technical - it "contains performance values of the UKADR radars" - but then adds that the volume is "also a convenient place-holder for several other related findings" arising "out of earlier DI55 interest in any reliable information connected with this topic and focusing upon the potential technologies involved and their possible future military uses." That is an intelligence organisation stating on paper that it had a standing interest in the technology question.

Chapter 3 is the exploitation chapter. Its first paragraph states the purpose without hedging: to identify "any phenomena which could be robustly generated and provide a military advantage." What follows is redacted. Paragraph 2, "Exotic Vehicles", is remarkable for what a defence intelligence analyst was willing to commit to a classified page in 2000. It records that "the possibility of some sort of exotic vehicle, either terrestrial or from space could not be ruled out until all available evidence had been examined", that "the possibility of technologies beyond the scope of human engineering and conception could not initially be ruled out", and - critically - that had extra-terrestrial technology been proven, "then (possibly) recommendations could be made as to the way ahead." It goes further, sketching what would be required: propulsion "by the use of anti-matter, gravitational or torsion fields or of particle phenomena and fields or waves which are not currently known in science or technology", and reported performance that "appears to imply the negation of inertia". The chapter's stated conclusion is negative - "there is no indication that craft of extra-terrestrial origin exist" - but the surrounding architecture is that of a reverse-engineering feasibility study, and the section headed POTENTIAL APPLICATIONS, which announces "three possible related potential applications", has its entire page body struck out under Section 26 and remains struck out today.

Chapter 4 is the foreign-programmes chapter, and it is where the board's Soviet thread anchors. Paragraph 1 states: "It is clear that the Former Soviet Union has taken the study of UAPs seriously and that this may well be related to a programme of understanding and exploiting certain UAP-related natural phenomena, such as plasmas. A 'UFO' Commission was created under the USSR Union of Scientific and Engineering Societies in 1989, together in 1990 with an Inter-Industry Ufology Research Centre (SOYUZUFOSENTR) to co-ordinate records. This Centre had the rights of an Institute under the USSR Academy of Sciences, and was headed by V.G. AZAHAZHA (or AZHAZHA) an eminent researcher and former naval submariner." The paragraph goes on to record a ten-week school whose terms of reference were to study "ufology as a science of unidentified flying objects and their inter-relations with the universe and, in particular, with the biotechnosphere of the earth".

The chapter keeps naming things. Paragraph 2 identifies V.V. Alexandrov and cosmonaut Y.N. Glazov of the Cosmonaut Training Centre and the Scientific Production Association (Geophysics), and a supporting laboratory at Akhtubisk on the Volga. Paragraph 3, "Plasma Research", records that two Russian scientific papers summarised at Annex A appear to show "20-30 scientists have pursued research for the prevention of air target detection" - plasma stealth - and everything after that sentence is redacted under Section 26. Paragraph 5, "Near Field Effects", reports 1991 Russian soil sampling at alleged landing sites where "in all cases a local field developed over a period of three hours" and "impairment of human senses were reportedly observed in every person studied who had been 'in the field'". The DI55 analyst's own bracketed comment is the most telling line in the volume: "It is possible, and confirmed from some other data that russian scientists may have discovered some new field types."

The Executive Summary makes the intelligence posture explicit. Under "Foreign Military Interest", paragraph 9 records that UAP "are by no means confined to UK airspace", that "Other NATO nations, China, Russia and elsewhere frequently report the most extreme and puzzling incidents in the open press", and that DI55 had no access to any other government's classified reports. The identities of the countries known "to have at least one member of staff active in this area" are withheld under Section 27 - the international-relations exemption, not the defence one. Paragraph 10 adds that "Several governments have also been sufficiently concerned to set up Commissions and Institutes to examine the phenomena." The bullets on the following page state that Russian, Former Soviet Republic and Chinese authorities "have made a co-ordinated effort to understand the UAP topic", that "Several aircraft have been destroyed and at least four pilots have been killed 'chasing UFOs'", and that "Russian investigators have measured (or at least detected) 'fields', which are reported to have caused human effects when they are located close to the phenomena."

The redaction fight that followed is itself part of the record. The MoD's internal-review correspondence, held at The National Archives as DEFE 24/2090/1 and digitised, shows Clarke and Anthony challenging specific Volume 3 withholdings paragraph by paragraph, including Chapter 4 page 3 paragraph 12, where the reviewer notes that "information regarding DIS, the CIA desk officer and DI51 must remain redacted" while conceding that the Society for Scientific Exploration statement in the same sentence was already public. A CIA desk officer appears in the UK's classified UAP chapter on foreign programmes, and his existence is still redacted. That is the shape of the concealment, drawn from the inside.

### What it has denied

The MoD framed the whole exercise defensively: its own release note says the data "was studied principally to ascertain whether there is any evidence of a threat to the UK, and secondly, should the opportunity arise, to identify any potential military technologies of interest" - burying the exploitation objective in a subordinate clause. Volume 3 Chapter 3 paragraph 7 supplies the quotable disclaimer, "there is no indication that craft of extra-terrestrial origin exist", while the paragraphs enumerating the exploitation applications on the same and following pages remain blacked out under Section 26 two decades later.

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, disclosure, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- What are the "three possible related potential applications" identified in Chapter 3 paragraph 8? The entire page body and the following page are still withheld under Section 26.
- Which countries are named in Executive Summary paragraph 9 as having staff active on UAP? Withheld under Section 27, meaning disclosure was judged to damage international relations rather than defence.
- Who is the CIA desk officer referenced in Volume 3 Chapter 4 page 3 paragraph 12, and what was DI51's role? Both remain redacted after internal review.
- The released 48-page Volume 3 file examined here ends mid-Chapter 4; the contents page lists further sections on Former Soviet Union Aircraft Incidents, an FSU/Russian Experimental Vehicle, and OTHER NATIONAL ACTIVITY covering CHINA, SPAIN and USA & CANADA. The USA & Canada section was not reached in this pass and its contents are unverified here.
- What are the two Russian scientific papers summarised at Volume 3 Annex A, and are they identifiable in the open literature?
- The Volume 3 preface is dated February 2000 while the report as a whole is commonly described as completed in December 2000; the discrepancy between the volume's own date stamp and the cited completion date is unresolved.
- Condign's stated conclusion is that no extra-terrestrial craft exist, yet Chapter 3 is structured as a feasibility study for exploiting effects the same chapter says cannot be replicated. Whether that structure reflects a genuine analytic dead end or a compartment the author could not write into an at-most-SECRET volume is not settled by the released text.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 186 of 1111, score 46). 7 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C1/A2/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Chapter 3 paragraph 2 states that "the possibility of some sort of exotic vehicle, either terrestrial or from space could not be ruled out until all available evidence had been examined", that "the possibility of technologies beyond the scope of human engineering and conception could not initially be ruled out", and that if extra-terrestrial technology were proven "then (possibly) recommendations could be made as to the way ahead"; paragraph 3 lists candidate technologies including "propulsion by the use of anti-matter, gravitational or torsion fields", and paragraph 2 states the reported performance "appears to imply the negation of inertia". ## NASA/TM-2004-213082, 'Prospects for Breakthrough Propulsion from Physics' (2004)

NASA/TM-2004-213082, 'Prospects for Breakthrough Propulsion from Physics' (2004) is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2004-06 to 2004; axes C0 A0 D1; 7 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Marc G. Millis's summary of NASA's Breakthrough Propulsion Physics Project, which adjudicates Biefeld-Brown, lifters, asymmetrical capacitors, Podkletnov, metric engineering and high frequency gravitational waves by name.

### What it is

NASA/TM-2004-213082, 'Prospects for Breakthrough Propulsion from Physics', by Marc G. Millis, the summary report of NASA's Breakthrough Propulsion Physics Project. It adjudicates, in one openable 19-page document with named references, the three claims this board's propulsion strand is built around: Biefeld-Brown and lifters, metric engineering, and the experimental record behind both.

### Why it is implicated

It is the closest thing this subject has to a referee. A national space agency ran a project specifically to assess breakthrough propulsion claims, published its findings under a named author with a reference list, and reached conclusions that are quotable and checkable. Where the lore says a mechanism is suppressed, this document says what was tested and what the tests found.

### The longer account

The most useful sentence on this board's propulsion question is in section 3.2.7 of this report: that in 1928 a device was patented for creating thrust using high-voltage capacitors, that many variants followed, and that **all rigorous experimental tests indicate the observed thrust is attributable to ion wind**, with the devices not viable candidates. Behind that phrase NASA prints its three references, including Talley's PL-TR-91-3009 final report for the Air Force, so the adjudication is not an assertion but a citation.

Section 3.3.1 does the same for metric engineering, recording that the concepts face enormous technical hurdles and in particular require enormous quantities of negative energy. That is the same finding the DIA warp-drive document reaches independently and quantifies.

The report is also useful for dating a term. NASA recorded in 2004 that 'metric engineering' had emerged at aerospace conferences and noted where it came from, which matters because the UAP literature treats the phrase as established technical vocabulary rather than as a recent coinage from a specific community.

And it supplies one of the two checkable facts behind this board's treatment of the Hutchison claim, by word count over its complete text. The board's position on Hutchison is that it rests entirely on undocumented video, and a NASA assessment that surveyed the field without engaging it is part of why.

A caution the node carries: this is a 2004 document. It is Tier 1 for what NASA concluded in 2004 from the experiments it cites, and it is not a standing refutation of work done since. The Army Research Laboratory dissent on the ion-wind mechanism, recorded in this cluster's conflicts, is an example of the argument continuing among people doing real work.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Talley's PL-TR-91-3009 itself, which NASA cites as one of the three experimental references. It returned HTTP 403 and was not opened.
- Tajmar 2004, the other reference behind the ion-wind finding, also not opened.
- Whether NASA or any successor has revisited the Biefeld-Brown adjudication since 2004.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 400 of 1111, score 34). 7 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A0/D1.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Section 3.3.2 treats high frequency gravitational waves as a DETECTION problem, opening 'Fundamentally, gravitational waves are perturbations in spacetime caused by violent accelerations of large masses, such as collisions of black holes' and proceeding to interferometry and LIGO. Fifteen years later the US Navy was granted US 10,322,827 for a high frequency gravitational wave GENERATOR. Both statements are Tier 1 for what their own documents say. Source: NASA/TM-2004-213082, Marc G. Millis, 'Prospects for Breakthrough Propulsion from Physics', NASA Glenn Research Center, 19 pages, https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/20040070788/downloads/20040070788.pdf, opened 2026-09-19
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE BIEFELD-BROWN ADJUDICATION, section 3.2.7, in NASA's own words: 'all rigorous experimental tests indicate that the observed thrust is attributable to ion wind', and the devices are 'not viable candidates'. NASA prints three references behind that phrase, including Talley, R. L., 'Twenty First Century Propulsion Concept', PL-TR-91-3009. Source: NASA/TM-2004-213082 section 3.2.7, opened 2026-09-19
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE BIEFELD-BROWN ADJUDICATION, section 3.2.7, verbatim and in full: 'In 1928 a device was patented for creating thrust using high-voltage capacitors. Since then, a wide variety of variants of this "Biefeld-Brown" effect, such as "Lifters" and "Asymmetrical Capacitors" have claimed that such devices operate on an "electrostatic antigravity" or "electrogravitic" effect. One of the most recent variants was patented by NASA-MSFC. To date, all rigorous experimental tests indicate that the observed thrust is attributable to ion wind. Vacuum tests currently underway, sponsored through an additional Congressional earmark to the West Virginia Institute for Scientific Research, also indicate that this effect is not indicative of new propulsion physics. These tests are now assessing the more conventional performance of such devices. These "Biefeld-Brown," "Lifter" and "Asymmetrical Capacitor Thrusters" are not viable candidates for breakthrough physics propulsion.' Source: NASA/TM-2004-213082, Marc G. Millis, 'Prospects for Breakthrough Propulsion from Physics', NASA Glenn Research Center, 19 pages, https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/20040070788/downloads/20040070788.pdf, opened 2026-09-19
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE HUTCHISON ZERO-COUNT, and it is one of the two checkable facts behind this board's treatment of that claim. Word counts over the complete 19-page text: Biefeld 4, Brown 6, lifter 2, asymmetric 5, Podkletnov 12, Alcubierre 1, ion wind 1, and HUTCHISON 0. NASA's project existed to triage claims of exactly this kind and found about a third of sixteen tasks not viable; the Hutchison effect did not reach the list of things worth finding not viable. THIS IS EVIDENCE ABOUT WHAT NASA EXAMINED AND IS NOT A FINDING THAT THE CLAIM IS FALSE. Source: NASA/TM-2004-213082, Marc G. Millis, 'Prospects for Breakthrough Propulsion from Physics', NASA Glenn Research Center, 19 pages, https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/20040070788/downloads/20040070788.pdf, opened 2026-09-19
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE METRIC-ENGINEERING ADJUDICATION, section 3.3.1, verbatim: 'Enormous technical hurdles face these concepts. In particular, they require enormous quantities of "negative energy" (equivalent mass of planets or suns), and evoke time-travel paradoxes ("closed-time-like curves").' And: 'Recently, the term "metric engineering" has emerged at aerospace conferences to represent such space-warping propulsion concepts. The origin of this term is unknown. Given the magnitude of energy requirements to create perceptible effects, it is unlikely that experimental work will be forthcoming in the near future. Even though these theoretical concepts are extremely unlikely to be engineered, they are at least useful as teaching tools to more thoroughly explore the intricacies of Einstein's General Relativity.' Source: NASA/TM-2004-213082, Marc G. Millis, 'Prospects for Breakthrough Propulsion from Physics', NASA Glenn Research Center, 19 pages, https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/20040070788/downloads/20040070788.pdf, opened 2026-09-19
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE METRIC-ENGINEERING ADJUDICATION, section 3.3.1, verbatim: 'Enormous technical hurdles face these concepts. In particular, they require enormous quantities of "negative energy"'. NASA and the DIA warp-drive document reach the same finding independently, and the DIA document quantifies it. Source: NASA/TM-2004-213082 section 3.3.1, opened 2026-09-19
- **Tier 1 documented.** The abstract's own statement of the project's result: 'To assess the implications of these emerging prospects for future spaceflight, NASA supported the Breakthrough Propulsion Physics Project from 1996 through 2002... Of 16 incremental research tasks completed by the project and from other sponsors, about a third were found not to be viable, a quarter have clear opportunities for sequels, and the rest remain unresolved.' Source: NASA/TM-2004-213082, Marc G. Millis, 'Prospects for Breakthrough Propulsion from Physics', NASA Glenn Research Center, 19 pages, https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/20040070788/downloads/20040070788.pdf, opened 2026-09-19
- **Tier 1 documented.** The three references NASA gives behind 'all rigorous experimental tests', as printed: Talley, R. L., 'Twenty First Century Propulsion Concept,' PL-TR-91-3009, Final Report for the period Feb 89 to July 90, on Contract FO4611-89-C-0023, Phillips Laboratory, Air Force Systems Command, Edwards AFB, CA (1991); Tajmar, M., 'Experimental Investigation of 5-D Divergent Currents as a Gravity-Electromagnetism Coupling Concept,' AIP Conference Proceedings, 504, STAIF, Albuquerque, NM, (2000), pp. 998-1003; and Tajmar, M., 'The Biefeld-Brown Effect: Misinterpretation of Corona Wind Phenomena,' AIAA Journal of Propulsion and Power, 42, (2004), pp. 315-318. NONE OF THOSE THREE WAS OPENED BY THIS SESSION and none is asserted from directly. Source: NASA/TM-2004-213082, Marc G. - NASA/TM-2004-213082, 'Prospects for Breakthrough Propulsion from Physics' (2004) runs out to **US Patent 10,322,827 - High frequency gravitational wave generator** (document, board tier C), a research link at Tier 3 stated inference. The link reads: NASA treated high frequency gravitational waves as a detection problem in 2004. STATED INFERENCE ABOUT THE DISTANCE BETWEEN TWO DOCUMENTS AND NOT A REFUTATION. Section 3.3.2 opens 'Fundamentally, gravitational waves are perturbations in spacetime caused by violent accelerations of large masses' and proceeds to interferometry and LIGO. Fifteen years later US 10,322,827 was granted for a generator. Both statements are Tier 1 for what their own documents say and nothing opened joins them. Sources: NASA/TM-2004-213082, Marc G. Millis, 'Prospects for Breakthrough Propulsion from Physics', NASA Glenn Research Center, 19 pages, https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/20040070788/downloads/20040070788.pdf, dated 2004.

## US Patent 10,144,532 - Craft using an inertial mass reduction device

US Patent 10,144,532 - Craft using an inertial mass reduction device is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2016-04-28 to 2023-01-09; axes C2 A1 D0; 6 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Application 15/141270, filed 28 April 2016, granted 4 December 2018; assignee the Secretary of the Navy.

### What it is

United States Patent 10,144,532, 'Craft using an inertial mass reduction device', application 15/141,270, filed 28 April 2016 from the Naval Air Warfare Center Aircraft Division and granted 4 December 2018, assigned to 'The United States of America as represented by the Secretary of the Navy' under Navy docket PAX 205. The examiner was Philip J. Bonzell, supervised by Timothy Collins; the application moved from Art Unit 3644 to Art Unit 3642 mid-prosecution without changing examiner. The patent expired on 9 January 2023 for non-payment of the first maintenance fee under 37 CFR 1.362.

### Why it is implicated

It is the control case for the patents tab. A Navy-assigned propulsion filing that did issue is what makes an absence elsewhere in the patent record a legal artefact of the Invention Secrecy Act rather than evidence that nothing exists. It is also the filing that produced the public controversy, and the file wrapper contradicts the story most often told about it. The secondary accounts say the application was rejected and then granted after the Naval Aviation Enterprise's chief technology officer asserted operability and invoked Chinese development of comparable technology. The wrapper shows the examiner refusing that argument in writing, and shows the grant arriving through a formal appeal rather than a letter.

### The longer account

The prosecution record is unusually explicit for a document of this kind, and it is worth following in order. Non-final rejection 28 November 2017. A telephonic examiner interview on 17 January 2018 at which, in the record's words, 'no agreement was reached'. Final rejection 30 March 2018. A response after final on 24 May 2018 carrying a declaration under 37 C.F.R. 1.132 signed by Dr James Sheehy on 11 May 2018. Notice of Appeal 21 June 2018, on form PTO/AIA/33, an 800 dollar fee against deposit account 600508. Advisory Action 11 July 2018 refusing to allow. Appeal Brief 21 August 2018, re-filing the same 11 May declaration in its Evidence Appendix rather than a second one. Reasons for Allowance 24 October 2018, Notice of Allowance 31 October, grant 4 December. The examiner allowed the application before the Patent Trial and Appeal Board ever ruled.

The examiner quantified his objection rather than gesturing at it. Polarising a quantum vacuum as claimed 'would take 10^9 teslas and 10^18V/m', which 'are not feasible with current technology', and: 'The largest magnetic field ever created is 10^3 teslas and a neutron star is 10^6 teslas so how are you using a microwave emitter that produces a magnetic field that is three orders of magnitude greater than a neutron star?' The same passage repeats verbatim in the final rejection. He also rejected the physical premise in terms, writing that there is no such thing as a repulsive electromagnetic energy field and that electromagnetism can be repulsive in certain circumstances but there is no blanket field that repels everything.

On the argument the secondary accounts credit with winning the patent, the wrapper is unambiguous: 'With respect to third argument that China is already investing significantly in this area, this has nothing to do with what is patentable. If anyone else filed this Application they would receive the same rejection.' Peer review fared no better. On the AIAA and SAE presentations the examiner wrote that presentation and committee review 'does not equate to the device being patentable', and that 'Presenting math at a conference doesn't prove anything physical.'

What was eventually granted is narrow. Claim 1 is the only independent claim and recites an inner resonant cavity wall, an outer resonant cavity wall and microwave emitters creating high frequency electromagnetic waves that vibrate the outer wall in an accelerated mode and create a local polarized vacuum outside it. There are four claims in total. Five years later the Navy stopped paying for it.

### What it has denied

The denial on this node runs in the unusual direction. The Navy did not deny anything about this filing; a federal examiner denied, on the record and in quantified terms, that the claimed effect is physically achievable, and did so twice before the applicant appealed. That is the only adjudicated technical opinion this board holds on any of the Pais filings, and it is negative. Two further things follow, and both matter for how the node should be read. The allowance that eventually issued came from the same examiner rather than from the Patent Trial and Appeal Board, so no appellate body has ever endorsed the disclosure. And the board's own edge from this patent to the five observables is graded Tier 2 and describes what the briefing claims the device would deliver, not a capability anyone has demonstrated.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the Navy's failure to pay the first maintenance fee was a decision or an oversight is not established. Nothing on this board explains the expiry.
- Whether the Notice of Appeal was ever docketed at the Patent Trial and Appeal Board is not established. The examiner allowed the application first, so no PTAB decision exists to read.
- The node's own description still says the correspondence asserting operability has not been obtained for this board, while its claims cite the Sheehy letter of 15 December 2017 and the declaration of 11 May 2018 as transcribed under research/patents/wrapper/. The description is behind the claims and should be reconciled.
- No test data, experimental result or hardware associated with the device has been located. The board holds the paper record of the filing and nothing else.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 62 of 1111, score 74). 6 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A1/D0.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** A PATENT IS TIER 1 FOR WHAT IT CLAIMS AND NEVER FOR WHETHER THE THING WORKS. Claim 1, as printed: 'A craft using an inertial mass reduction device comprising: an inner resonant cavity wall; an outer resonant cavity wall, the inner resonant cavity wall and the outer resonant cavity wall forming a resonant cavity; and, microwave emitters such that the microwave emitters create high frequency electromagnetic waves throughout the resonant cavity causing the outer resonant cavity wall to vibrate in an accelerated mode and create a local polarized vacuum outside the outer resonant cavity wall.' The Navy's own test of the related High Energy Electromagnetic Field Generator concluded in September 2019 that the effect could not be proven. There are four claims in total. # 2017-2026: Post-disclosure

## AARO FY2025 Consolidated Annual Report on UAP

AARO FY2025 Consolidated Annual Report on UAP is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2026; axes C1 A3 D3; 18 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Released 21 July 2026, months after the statutory cycle.

### What it is

The Fiscal Year 2025 Consolidated Annual Report on Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena is the fourteen-page statutory report submitted by the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office under 50 U.S.C. § 3373(k), covering UAP events from 2 June 2024 to 30 May 2025, bearing the clearance number 26-P-0880 and issued under the letterhead "Department of War." It was publicly released on 21 July 2026, months after the annual reporting cycle Congress contemplated. It records 319 new reports, 370 total resolutions, and no unexplained residue attributed to advanced foreign or non-human capability.

### Why it is implicated

This is the current, dated instrument of official denial, and it is therefore the most useful single artifact on the board. Its flat assertion, "To date, no evidence suggests that a USG or private entity has ever captured or exploited UAP-derived materials", is immediately followed by the statement that AARO "is currently developing a formalized process for handling UAP-derived materials by an appropriate line organization, should such materials ever come into the possession of the USG," a process AARO says draws on established procedures governing the recovery of foreign materiel. An office that has found nothing is building a custody chain for it. The report also claims an access authority that collides directly with standing DoD regulation: AARO states it is authorized to receive UAP information "regardless of any restrictive access controls, special access controls, or compartmented access programs," while DoDD 5205.07 §3.2.g simultaneously forbids any DoD contractor employee from providing SAP material to Congress without SAPCO approval and 32 CFR 117.6 removes DCSA visibility from carved-out programs entirely. Claiming access is not the same as exercising it. And the report is internally inconsistent on its headline finding: §IV.C states AARO resolved 44 events as satellite flaring during the reporting period while §VI states the new capability "enabled analysts to resolve 238 reported UAP as satellite flaring."

### The longer account

The arithmetic is the place to start, because the report's own numbers do not reconcile cleanly and the discrepancy runs through its most-promoted achievement. AARO received 319 reports between 2 June 2024 and 30 May 2025: 284 events inside the window and 35 from 2010 to 2024 not previously reported. Of the 319, AARO resolved 114 and additionally resolved 256 cases from prior periods, for 370 resolutions in the reporting period. Case holdings totaled 1,870 as of 30 May 2025. By domain, 274 were airborne, 44 space, one maritime. Of the 319, 191 were transferred to the "active archive", the category for cases with insufficient data to assess whether performance characteristics are consistent with natural phenomena or exceed the known state of the art. Nine reports were identified as meriting further analysis by intelligence community and science-and-technology partners. Two case files were merged.

Now the flaring numbers. Section III.A states that all 44 space-domain reports were assessed as satellite flaring, of which 42 came from FAA civilian pilot reports and two from ground-based U.S. Space Command sensors, and notes, pointedly, that "none of AARO's space-domain case assessments originated from space-based sensors." Section IV.C states: "AARO resolved 44 UAP-related events as attributable to satellite flaring during the reporting period." Section VI states: "This capability, consisting of advanced three-dimensional modeling and simulation applications, enabled analysts to resolve 238 reported UAP as satellite flaring." The executive summary uses 238. Gillibrand's press release uses 238. The 238 figure only coheres if it spans the 256 previously reported cases as well as the current-period 44, which the report never states and which would mean the office's flagship analytic advance was applied overwhelmingly to a backlog rather than to new reporting. A downstream analyst should treat the 238/44 gap as an unresolved presentation problem in a fourteen-page document.

The substantive denials come in Section IV.G and Section IV.H. On materials: "To date, no evidence suggests that a USG or private entity has ever captured or exploited UAP-derived materials. AARO is currently developing a formalized process for handling UAP-derived materials by an appropriate line organization, should such materials ever come into the possession of the USG. AARO's development process draws on established USG capabilities and operating procedures governing the recovery of foreign materiel." On health: "To date, AARO has not received a UAP report in which a reporter described sustaining adverse health effects associated with their experience." Both are negative claims about AARO's own holdings, not about the world. The report never asserts that no such program or materials exist; it asserts that no evidence of them has reached this office.

The access claim in Section V.C is the report's strongest institutional assertion and its most testable: "AARO is authorized by law to receive all UAP-related information, including any classified national security information involving military or intelligence related activities, at any level of classification, regardless of any restrictive access controls, special access controls, or compartmented access programs. Generally, no restrictions, including non-disclosure agreements, prevent AARO from receiving UAP-related information, regardless of the organizational affiliation of the original classification authority within the DoW, the IC, or any other USG department or agency." Note the hedge: "Generally." Note also what the sentence does not say, it describes a right to receive, not an exercised capability to compel, and it does not state that AARO has audited any carved-out special access program. Against that, 262 individuals used the secure reporting mechanism in the period; AARO determined 255 were out of scope and identified seven for follow-on interview. A 97 percent out-of-scope rate is either an accurate measure of noise or a measure of a scoping filter, and the report gives no criteria by which to tell.

Two passages cut against the report's own framing. Section IV.E: "AARO received and analyzed several reports describing UAP in or near national security sites or critical infrastructure. Some narrative reports were suggestive of phenomena whose performance characteristics exceed the known state-of-the-art within a given domain. No technical data accompanied these reports. If validated, the attendant phenomena underlying these reports may represent a currently unmitigated threat vector." And Section III.A, the sole maritime case: a report from U.S. Navy assets operating off the coast of Virginia describing "approximately 100 airborne UAP and two, likely uncrewed, surface systems," which AARO says it is actively investigating. An office reporting no flight-safety concerns and no anomalous residue is simultaneously reporting an unmitigated threat vector and a hundred-object maritime event under investigation.

Section IV.F is the nuclear line. AARO received 50 reports from the Administrator for Nuclear Security and the Chairman of the Nuclear Regulatory Commission regarding unmanned aerial system incidents near U.S. nuclear infrastructure, weapons and launch sites, a 177.8 percent increase over the 18 in the FY2024 report. "None of these incidents were reported as UAP." Forty-seven of the 50 occurred between 1700 and 0500 local; five persisted at least an hour, the longest about three hours; 33 involved a single system, 17 involved between two and seven. The categorical exclusion of all 50 from UAP status is a classification decision made by the reporting agencies, not an analytic finding by AARO, and the report does not say who made it.

On process: the report was released 21 July 2026 under a statute requiring submission "not later than 180 days after December 23, 2022, and annually thereafter for four years." The FY2024 report was published in November 2024 and documented 757 reports against FY2025's 319. Senator Kirsten Gillibrand, who created AARO in the FY2022 NDAA and secured the FY2024 NDAA declassification language, issued a same-day statement calling the report "another crucial step forward" and AARO "the most comprehensive government effort on UAPs to date," and framed the arithmetic as 319 received, 114 explained, "leaving 205 unresolved." The report's own foreign-engagement section names exactly one activity for the year: AARO's deputy director presented a mission briefing to the Defense Geospatial Intelligence conference in London.

### What it has denied

The report issues two categorical denials and one implicit one. On materials: "To date, no evidence suggests that a USG or private entity has ever captured or exploited UAP-derived materials." On health: "To date, AARO has not received a UAP report in which a reporter described sustaining adverse health effects." On adversary technology: "None of the cases AARO resolved indicate advanced foreign adversarial capabilities or breakthrough technologies in any domain." Each is scoped to what has reached AARO rather than to what exists, and each is undercut within the same document by the unmitigated-threat-vector language, the hundred-object Virginia maritime case, and the fact that AARO is building a materials custody process for materials it says do not exist.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, concealment, oversight, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Why does §IV.C state 44 satellite-flaring resolutions for the reporting period while §VI and the Executive Summary state 238? The report never reconciles the figures or states which case population the 238 spans.
- Who decided that all 50 nuclear-adjacent UAS incidents were "not reported as UAP", the reporting agencies or AARO, and against what criteria?
- What are the scoping criteria by which 255 of 262 first-hand claimants were ruled out of scope? None are published.
- AARO says it is building a formalized process for handling UAP-derived materials "by an appropriate line organization." Which organization, under what authority, and with what facility?
- AARO claims access regardless of special access controls. Has AARO ever invoked that authority against a specific carved-out SAP, and if so, was access granted or refused?
- The February 2025 Joint Staff UAP GENADMIN remains classified. What does it direct, and does it reach contractor personnel?
- The Virginia maritime case describes approximately 100 airborne UAP and two likely uncrewed surface systems. What sensors recorded it, and what is the current status of the investigation?
- Two reports described electronic or avionic interference near operational aircraft; AARO says it has "not yet rendered a determination." What is the determination timeline, and which airframes were involved?
- The report was released roughly eight months later than the prior year's. What accounts for the delay, and was the report submitted to Congress on time even if released late?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 19 of 1111, score 110). 19 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 6 official denials recorded against it. Axes C1/A3/D3.
