## Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force (UAPTF)

Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force (UAPTF) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2020 to 2022; axes C2 A3 D2; 16 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Navy/ONI task force 2020-2022.

### What it is

The Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force (UAPTF) was a Department of Defense body approved by Deputy Secretary of Defense David L. Norquist on 4 August 2020 and announced publicly on 14 August 2020, led by the Department of the Navy under the cognizance of the Office of the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security. Its stated mission was to detect, analyze and catalog UAP that could potentially pose a threat to U.S. national security. It produced the dataset behind the June 2021 ODNI preliminary assessment and was superseded in November 2021 by AOIMSG and in 2022 by AARO.

### Why it is implicated

The UAPTF is where the modern disclosure fight acquired its documents and its witnesses. David Grusch served as the National Reconnaissance Office representative to the UAPTF, and it was from inside that role that he says he was briefed on, and then denied access to, a multi-decade crash retrieval and reverse-engineering program. That is the specific institutional pathway by which a legacy program's existence leaked into a body that Congress could subpoena. The task force also demonstrates the concealment mechanism in miniature: a Navy-led, OUSD(I&S)-supervised body with an unclassified public face and a classified annex, stood up only after decades of the same phenomena being handled through channels Congress was not shown. Within fifteen months of its public announcement it was dissolved and its functions moved twice, each time into a differently named organization with different reporting lines. Reorganization is a documented concealment technique on this board, and the UAPTF-to-AOIMSG-to-AARO chain is the cleanest recent example of it.

### The longer account

The establishment is on the record and unambiguous. The Navy's own press release of 14 August 2020 states: "On Aug. 4, 2020, Deputy Secretary of Defense David L. Norquist approved the establishment of an Unidentified Aerial Phenomena (UAP) Task Force (UAPTF). The Department of the Navy, under the cognizance of the Office of the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security, will lead the UAPTF." The mission statement is "to detect, analyze and catalog UAPs that could potentially pose a threat to U.S. national security." Note what is absent: any reference to materials, recoveries, or prior programs. The public charter is a sensor-and-safety charter.

What the UAPTF actually produced was the dataset in the ODNI preliminary assessment delivered 25 June 2021, prepared in response to a reporting provision attached to the Intelligence Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2021. That nine-page unclassified assessment, with a classified annex, covers 144 reports from U.S. government sources between November 2004 and March 2021, states that most reports came from Navy and other military aviators, and identifies a small number of cases where UAP "appeared to remain stationary in winds aloft, move against the wind, maneuver abruptly, or move at considerable speed, without discernable means of propulsion." The assessment explicitly declines to attribute the phenomena and cites limited, inconsistent reporting as the principal analytic constraint. It is the government's own written acknowledgment that a body of unexplained aerospace observations exists inside its own sensor record.

The personnel question is where the task force becomes load-bearing for the program argument. David Grusch, an Air Force veteran and intelligence officer, served as the NRO representative to the UAPTF from 2019 to 2021 and then as his agency's co-lead for UAP and trans-medium object analysis at the National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency from 2021 to 2023. In 2022 he filed a complaint with the Intelligence Community Inspector General that was found credible and urgent for reporting to the congressional intelligence committees. On 26 July 2023, under oath before the House Oversight Subcommittee on National Security, the Border, and Foreign Affairs, Grusch testified that in the course of his official duties he was informed of a multi-decade crash retrieval and reverse-engineering program to which he was denied access, and that recoveries included non-human biologics. Whatever one concludes about the substance, the provenance is institutional: the claim entered the public record through a person whose job was to represent a national intelligence agency to a DoD task force.

The dissolution is as instructive as the founding. On 23 November 2021 Deputy Secretary of Defense Kathleen Hicks, in coordination with the Director of National Intelligence, directed the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security to establish the Airborne Object Identification and Management Synchronization Group as the successor to the Navy's UAPTF, with oversight from an Airborne Object Identification and Management Executive Council drawn from DoD and the intelligence community. Ronald Moultrie was the USD(I&S) of record. AOIMSG itself lasted months before being reconstituted in July 2022 as the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office. Three names, three charters, three sets of reporting relationships, in under two years, for the same subject matter. Each transition reset what Congress had been briefed on and who owed the briefing.

The naming precision matters here in the same way it matters for AATIP. The UAPTF was a Navy-led DoD task force with an OUSD(I&S) reporting chain. It was not a special access program, it did not have acquisition authority, and nothing in its public charter gives it custody of anything. That is precisely Grusch's point: he says the compartmented activity he learned about was adjacent to, and walled off from, the body Congress had been told was handling the subject. The UAPTF is therefore best read on this board not as the program but as the sensor that detected the program from inside the building.

Who matters by name: Norquist (approving authority), Hicks (dissolving authority), Moultrie (USD(I&S)), Grusch (NRO representative), and the ODNI and Navy officials who signed out the June 2021 assessment. What the record shows is a legitimate, publicly chartered analytic body producing a public product that concedes unexplained observations. What the record does not show is any UAPTF authority over, or access to, materials, and the absence of that authority in a body specifically created to be the government's answer on UAP is the anomaly worth pulling on.

### What it has denied

DoD has never denied the UAPTF existed; it published its own establishment. The denial operates one level down, at AARO, which states in its 2024 Historical Record Report Volume 1 that it has found no evidence of any U.S. reverse-engineering program and that interviewees, including those with UAPTF-adjacent access, 'likely' misinterpreted sensitive but conventional programs because 'none of the interviewees had firsthand knowledge.' The denial is an assertion about the mental states of named witnesses rather than a document production.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, concealment, research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- The 4 August 2020 Norquist approval memorandum itself has not been released in full. What does the charter say beyond the press release summary?
- Who led the UAPTF day to day, and what was the size and composition of its staff? DoD has never published a roster.
- What is in the classified annex to the 25 June 2021 ODNI assessment, and has it been provided to the House Oversight Committee?
- Was there an ONI-run UAP task force predating the 4 August 2020 formalization, and if so under what authority did it operate?
- Grusch says he was briefed on a program while serving as NRO representative to the UAPTF. Who briefed him, and does the UAPTF hold a record of that briefing?
- What happened to UAPTF holdings during the two reorganizations, and were any records destroyed or reclassified in transit?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 79 of 1111, score 69). 19 connections, 6 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A3/D2.

## The Galileo Project

The Galileo Project is a program on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2021; axes C1 A0 D3; 9 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Harvard-based unclassified UAP and interstellar-object instrumentation programme, founded July 2021.

### What it is

The Galileo Project for the Systematic Scientific Search for Evidence of Extraterrestrial Technological Artifacts is a Harvard-hosted, cross-institutional research programme launched in July 2021 and co-founded by Avi Loeb, the Frank B. Baird Jr. Professor of Science at Harvard, and Frank Laukien, chairman, president and CEO of the scientific instrument manufacturer Bruker Corporation. It operates three branches: the study of interstellar objects near Earth, the search for unidentified aerial phenomena using multimodal ground-based observatories, and the study of interstellar meteors. It is funded entirely by private donation.

### Why it is implicated

Galileo is the control experiment for this entire board. Every version of the legacy-program hypothesis and every official denial of it reduce to claims about physical evidence held under classification. The only structural way to break that is an independent, uncleared, publishing instrument capable of producing calibrated data on the same phenomena - data that is not subject to prepublication classification review because it was never collected on government money or on government sensors. Galileo is the first serious institutional attempt to build one, and its funding structure is the mechanism: $1.755 million raised in the first two weeks from named private donors, which means its outputs go to arXiv rather than to a DOPSR queue. Its director also co-authored a March 2023 draft paper with Sean Kirkpatrick, the sitting Director of the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office - the only documented instance of a joint technical product between the Pentagon's UAP office and an independent academic programme. And its IM1 interstellar meteor work is the most instructive public failure mode on the board: a physical-recovery campaign that produced material, published it, and could not establish a chain from the material to the event, generating a citation war that maps precisely onto the epistemics of the whole subject.

### The longer account

Loeb's framing paper, "Overview of the Galileo Project," was posted to arXiv on 2 August 2022 as arXiv:2209.02479 and published in a special issue of the Journal of Astronomical Instrumentation in 2023. Its abstract describes the project as "the first systematic scientific research program in search for potential astro-archaeological artifacts or remnants of extraterrestrial technological civilizations (ETCs) or potentially active equipment near Earth," and characterises the approach as taking a path not taken and conceivably picking low-hanging fruit. That is a statement about method, and the method is the point: Galileo's premise is that the subject has been starved of instrumented, calibrated, continuous data, not of anecdotes.

The UAP branch built to that premise. Wesley Watters and colleagues published "The Scientific Investigation of Unidentified Aerial Phenomena (UAP) Using Multimodal Ground-Based Observatories" in the Journal of Astronomical Instrumentation, volume 12, article 2340006 (2023), laying out the sensor architecture: synchronised optical, infrared, radio, magnetometric and acoustic channels producing a continuous census of everything above an observation site, so that anomaly is defined by exclusion from a measured baseline rather than by the surprise of an observer. The first instrument came online at Harvard. Commissioning results for the all-sky infrared camera array were posted as arXiv:2411.07956, and a system architecture paper followed as arXiv:2506.00125. Loeb has publicly discussed commissioning data covering roughly half a million objects. No Galileo detection has been shown to be non-human technology, and the project has not claimed otherwise.

The interstellar meteor branch is where the project collided with the discipline. CNEOS 2014-01-08, designated IM1, is a bolide detected over the western Pacific whose inferred velocity implied a hyperbolic, interstellar trajectory. In summer 2023 Galileo mounted an ocean expedition off Papua New Guinea, dragging a magnetic sled across a roughly ten-kilometre region around the fireball localisation across twenty-six runs and recovering approximately 700 sub-millimetre spherules. Loeb and colleagues reported that a subset had a distinctive composition rich in beryllium, lanthanum and uranium - the 'BeLaU' type - which they argued was inconsistent with solar-system materials. The chemical classification work was eventually published in Chemical Geology (2024), with the preprint at arXiv:2401.09882.

The response was immediate and formal, and it is worth cataloguing precisely because it is the model of how a materials claim gets adjudicated in the open. Steve Desch and Alan Jackson published a direct critique, arXiv:2311.07699, arguing among other things that the spherules plot on or extremely near a terrestrial fractionation line and that a body entering at the assumed IM1 velocity would have been essentially vaporised, so that recovered iron spherules cannot be attributed to that event. A separate paper, arXiv:2403.05161, proposed the BeLaU signature as microtektites of terrestrial laterites. Most damaging to the localisation, Fernando and colleagues published "Seismic and acoustic signals from the 2014 'interstellar meteor'" in Geophysical Journal International, volume 238, issue 2 (2024), concluding that the seismic signal used to pin the impact site changed direction over time in a way that matched a road running past the seismometer - a truck, not a bolide. Galileo replied with "Peak-Brightness Localization of the CNEOS 2014-01-08 (IM1) Fireball," arXiv:2403.07696, arguing that the search region was set by the NASA/JPL CNEOS localisation box - 11.112 km on a side, centred at 1.3 degrees south, 147.6 degrees east - and not by the seismic data at all.

Whichever way that dispute resolves, its structure is the finding. A privately funded team recovered candidate material, published composition data, and could not establish provenance linking material to event; independent groups published falsifying analyses within months; the original team responded in the same channel. This is exactly the adjudication process that has never been available for any material alleged to be in government custody, and the contrast is the argument. The Nolan-Vallee 2022 paper faces the same problem from the other direction - a sample with a documented same-night recovery but a forty-four-year custody gap.

The most direct institutional connection on the board is the Loeb-Kirkpatrick paper. Dated 7 March 2023 and titled "Physical Constraints on Unidentified Aerial Phenomena," it derives physical constraints on interpretations of highly manoeuvrable UAP from standard physics and known forms of matter and radiation, and it is co-authored by the serving Director of AARO. The draft circulated publicly, drew criticism from multiple directions, and was never formally issued as an AARO product. It nonetheless establishes that the Pentagon's UAP office and Galileo were in direct technical collaboration during Kirkpatrick's tenure, which bears on how one reads AARO's later categorical statements and on what data, if any, moved in either direction.

For an analyst, Galileo's value is threefold. It produces a public, timestamped, instrumented baseline that any future disclosure has to be consistent with. Its funding graph - Eugene Jhong, Vinny Jain, Teddy Jones, Eric Keto, the Laukien Science Foundation, Joerg Laukien, William A. Linton, Adnan Sen, the Brinson Foundation - identifies the private capital willing to underwrite the question outside the appropriations system. And through Laukien and Bruker it sits one step from the mass-spectrometry instrument industry whose products define what any materials claim, government or otherwise, can actually be tested with.

### What it has denied

Galileo issues no denials; it is the party against which official denial is directed. Its own posture is one of null results honestly reported - no Galileo observation has been shown to be non-human technology, and the project has repeatedly declined to assert probabilities. That posture is itself evidentially useful, because it establishes that the project's later claims, if any, would not be the product of a group predisposed to affirm.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether Galileo observatory data has ever been requested by, shared with, or offered to AARO under 50 U.S.C. 3373, and whether any such exchange is documented
- What became of the Loeb-Kirkpatrick draft paper - whether it was ever submitted, reviewed, or suppressed, and whether AARO reviewed it as an official product
- Whether the BeLaU spherules can be independently re-analysed by a laboratory with no stake in the outcome, and whether sample material has been distributed
- Whether any Galileo observatory site has been sited near locations with historical UAP incident density, and how site selection was made
- Whether the project has retained legal or classification advice regarding what would happen to its data if a detection were judged to implicate a classified US programme
- The extent of overlap between Galileo's donor network and the private funders of other UAP research vehicles

## Nolan, Vallee, Jiang & Lemke (2022), Progress in Aerospace Sciences 128:100788

Nolan, Vallee, Jiang & Lemke (2022), Progress in Aerospace Sciences 128:100788 is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2022; axes C2 A0 D2; 8 connections; in the research band.

In one line: 'Improved instrumental techniques, including isotopic analysis, applicable to the characterization of unusual materials with potential relevance to aerospace forensics'.

### What it is

A peer-reviewed review article, "Improved instrumental techniques, including isotopic analysis, applicable to the characterization of unusual materials with potential relevance to aerospace forensics," by Garry P. Nolan, Jacques F. Vallee, Sizun Jiang and Larry G. Lemke, published in Elsevier's Progress in Aerospace Sciences, Volume 128, January 2022, article 100788 (online 9 December 2021), DOI 10.1016/j.paerosci.2021.100788. Its first half reviews mass-spectrometry and imaging methods for characterizing unknown solids; its second half applies them to a preserved sample, designated CB_JV-1, from the 17 December 1977 molten-metal fall at Council Bluffs, Iowa. It is the first materials-analysis paper on a UAP-associated sample to appear in a mainstream aerospace journal.

### Why it is implicated

This paper is the methodological hinge of the entire material-evidence question. It puts on the record, in a journal that describes itself as invitation-only and that is read by the aerospace engineering community, a replicable protocol for exactly the task a legacy exploitation program would have been performing in secret for decades: determining whether a recovered solid was manufactured, by whom, and by what process. Its lead author, Garry Nolan, is a Stanford professor of pathology whose analytical lab work brought him into contact with the contractor-adjacent UAP community; its second author, Jacques Vallee, holds the sample catalogue that has been the reference set for physical-trace UFO cases since the 1960s. The paper cites, on page 3, one of the Defense Intelligence Reference Documents produced under the DIA-funded Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications Program (AAWSAP) - Maxim Tsoi's "Metallic Spintronics" (2010) - which drags a government UAP-program artifact into the open Elsevier literature and into a citation graph that can be followed. Finally, the paper's actual result is the inconvenient one: isotope ratios consistent with terrestrial norms, but an inhomogeneity pattern the authors say would not be expected to form naturally and that they cannot source to any identified industrial process. That is the shape of an evidentiary problem, not a claim of proof - which is what makes it usable.

### The longer account

The Council Bluffs event is one of the very few physical-trace cases in the UFO record with a same-night, multi-agency chain of custody. At approximately 1945 CST on 17 December 1977, two residents of Council Bluffs, Iowa, watched a red luminous mass descend near the northern city limits. When they reached the site they found ground covered by molten metal glowing red-orange and igniting grass. Police and fire personnel were on scene within roughly fifteen minutes and all of them saw the mass, which they estimated at 35 to 55 pounds. Investigators at the time ruled out re-entering space debris, falling aircraft material, a meteorite (there was no crater), and a hoax. Two of the eleven witnesses described a round object hovering in the sky edged with red blinking lights. Vallee took possession of a fragment. Forty-four years later Nolan's Stanford laboratory - which had developed Multiplexed Ion Beam Imaging (MIBI), a mass-spectrometric imaging technique built for tissue but capable of resolving a much broader isotope range than the 1978-era instruments - ran it again.

The result was not what either camp wanted. The paper reports that all isotopic ratios were similar between the samples and did not show statistically significant deviations from expected terrestrial normal, with one exception involving 57Fe for which the authors themselves offer a conventional explanation. In other words: the sample is made of Earth atoms. What the paper does not do is stop there. The elemental map shows local homogeneity inside individual measured grains but pronounced inhomogeneity across subsamples, which the authors read as incomplete mixing - the fingerprint of a process, not a natural formation. Their published summary of the Council Bluffs material is that it shows no evidence of having been engineered or designed, would not be expected to form naturally, and has unusual inhomogeneity. The origin, handling history and delivery mechanism remain unidentified.

The hostile reading of the paper is worth cataloguing because it is specific and it is on the record. The most detailed public critique, a long-form analysis at Metabunk, argues that the four most abundant elements identified in the paper's Figure 9 - aluminium, silicon, iron and magnesium - are the 3rd, 2nd, 4th and 7th most abundant elements in Earth's crust and are all consistent with a thermite or improvised-thermite reaction; that the MIBI-SIMS configuration used cannot detect oxygen, which would be the discriminating element for a redox reaction; that two of five subsamples returned compromised ion counts; that the paper's citation of Southall and Oberly's 1979 paper on airborne superconducting generators does not support the liquid-metal and novel-propulsion inferences drawn from it; and that roughly 350 words are reused near-verbatim from Vallee's own 1998 paper "Physical Analyses in Ten Cases of Unexplained Aerial Objects with Material Samples" without being marked as reused. None of these critiques have produced a retraction, and none of them contradict the isotopic finding. They are, for an analyst, a checklist of what a replication attempt has to close out.

The reason this paper matters to a board about a concealed program is structural rather than evidentiary. Every version of the legacy-program hypothesis reduces, eventually, to a claim about custody of physical material. Every official denial reduces to a claim about the absence of such material. Both claims are testable by exactly the instruments described here, and only by them. The Nolan-Vallee paper is therefore the open-literature specification of the test that the government has consistently declined to submit its holdings to - and, notably, the government has since begun performing a shadow version of the same test on its own terms. AARO's FY2024 annual report to Congress states plainly that Oak Ridge National Laboratory "provided analysis for a material claimed to be from a UAP" and that AARO S&T, in coordination with ORNL, wrote and posted a materials-analysis report on its website, with supplemental data promised. That is the same workflow, run inside the fence, with the sample provenance and the raw data controlled by the party with the strongest institutional interest in the answer.

The analyst-facing question the paper leaves open is not whether CB_JV-1 is alien. The authors expressly refuse that framing, writing that the objective is to provide data in an open-source manner so that others might replicate the analytic approaches or derive a testable hypothesis for how such materials are deposited. The question is who else has run this protocol, on what, and where those results went. Nolan has stated publicly that he has analysed other samples; the paper itself notes further analyses in progress. The gap between the published Council Bluffs work and whatever else has passed through that instrument is one of the more tractable research targets on this board.

### What it has denied

The paper issues no denial and makes no claim of non-terrestrial origin; it explicitly refuses that framing and reports terrestrial isotope ratios. Its posture is one of deliberate under-claiming, which the authors themselves anticipate will feel "incomplete or insufficient" to readers on both sides.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- The chain of custody for CB_JV-1 between the 1977 recovery by Council Bluffs police/fire and Vallee's retained fragment is not documented in the public record
- The identity and institutional affiliation of 'J. Roser', quoted in both the 1998 and 2022 papers without citation, is unresolved
- What other samples the Nolan laboratory has run using this protocol, at whose request, and where those results went
- Whether the AARO/ORNL materials analysis used comparable isotopic methods, and whether its raw data has been released as promised
- Whether the thermite hypothesis raised in the Metabunk critique has ever been tested experimentally against the CB_JV-1 elemental profile

### Why it holds its tier

## Sky Canada Project - Office of the Chief Science Advisor of Canada

Sky Canada Project - Office of the Chief Science Advisor of Canada is a program on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2022 to 2025-06; axes C0 A0 D3; 8 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Canadian federal review of public UAP reporting, 2022-2025.

### What it is

A review conducted by the Office of the Chief Science Advisor of Canada into how public reports of unidentified aerial phenomena are handled by the Canadian federal government, published in June 2025 as a 59-page report titled 'Management of Public Reporting of Unidentified Aerial Phenomena in Canada,' carrying a message signed by Chief Science Advisor Mona Nemer. It surveyed federal departments, national and local police, civil aviation, the Coast Guard, citizen-science organizations and international comparators, and issued fourteen recommendations across reporting and data oversight, communications, research, and international collaboration.

### Why it is implicated

The Sky Canada Project is the board's cleanest documentation of concealment-by-non-recording, and it was written by a government about itself. Its central finding is that no Canadian federal body holds the UAP file: the RCMP has no policy, no classification and therefore no statistics; Transport Canada logs UAP into an aviation-safety database as an incidental category and does not investigate absent a safety concern; the RCAF does not investigate outside a threat or distress context; the National Research Council stopped collecting in 1995 and shipped fifteen thousand pages to the archives. Where no organization is responsible for a category of event, the absence of evidence is manufactured rather than observed, and every subsequent official statement that there is nothing to report becomes structurally true and evidentially worthless. The report also documents a measurable narrowing: direct submission of UAP reports from Canadian federal departments to independent researcher Chris Rutkowski, running since 2000, 'decreased significantly' by 2020, precisely as the American record was reopening. And it establishes the historical pattern, because Canada ran two named federal UAP programs, Project Magnet and Project Second Storey, and closed both on a committee finding that UFOs 'neither posed a security threat, nor were of scientific interest,' the Canadian twin of the Robertson Panel logic. Finally, it is an ally-government artifact that treats AARO as a model and recommends formal partnership with it, showing how the American framing is exported into allied policy.

### The longer account

Canada's federal engagement with the phenomenon is older than most assume and was twice institutionalized and twice shut down. In 1950 the Department of Transport granted engineer Wilbert Smith permission to research whether some UFOs might use the power of the Earth's magnetic field as a source of propulsion. That was Project Magnet. Smith expanded its scope to include validating UFO observations, launched a balloon over Ottawa to monitor public and media reaction, and established a UFO observatory outside the capital. The department terminated Project Magnet in 1954 and Smith continued privately. In 1952 the Defence Research Board established Project Second Storey, chaired by National Research Council astronomer Dr. Peter Millman with members from Canadian military intelligence and other agencies. Second Storey did not investigate sightings. It produced a reporting form that was never broadly adopted, met six times between 1952 and 1954, concluded that 'UFOs neither posed a security threat, nor were of scientific interest,' and recommended ending all investigations by the Canadian military. That recommendation held for seventy years.

From 1954 to 1967 reports drifted to the RCMP, Transport Canada, National Defence and local police, occasionally logged, never systematized. In 1967, on the recommendation of the Minister of National Defence, the file went to the National Research Council, a transfer that included declassification of both Second Storey and Magnet. Millman personally answered public queries, usually with stars, planets, meteors and optical phenomena. In 1995 the NRC stopped collecting altogether and everything went to Library and Archives Canada, where the collection sits today at an estimated fifteen thousand pages, of which some nine thousand five hundred have been digitized. Over the last decade the NRC has processed only about forty UAP-related inquiries.

That is the vacuum the Sky Canada Project stepped into, and its consultations produced admissions more useful than any sighting. The RCMP, which serves roughly seventy-three percent of Canada's landmass and holds a federal investigative mandate nationwide, told the OCSA it has no formal policy for collection or dissemination of UAP reports and no classification system specific to UAPs, and that such reports are typically categorized as non-offence incidents under 'Aeronautics Act - Other Activities,' a bucket that also holds drone sightings. There is no guidance for call takers; a UAP call can be logged as a 'suspicious vehicle' and lost. The report states the consequence in a single sentence: 'It was therefore impossible for the RCMP to provide statistics about UAP reports.' The RCMP nonetheless told the OCSA it saw potential value in a dedicated office to which UAP reports could be referred.

Civil aviation is barely better. Transport Canada maintains the Civil Aviation Daily Occurrence Reporting System, into which NAV CANADA feeds roughly eighty percent of the content, and UAP sightings land in a category labelled 'Weather Balloon, Meteor, Rocket, UFO and Intelligence Sighting.' The report cites CTVNews.ca's January 2024 review of CADORS identifying only seventeen 2023 events that could be considered UAP, about 0.08 percent of pilot-reported incidences, and states the consequence directly: 'They are so rare in CADORS that Transport Canada is not incentivized to dedicate time and effort to explaining them. In fact, when such incidents do not raise serious safety concerns, Canadian authorities do not investigate further.' The RCAF 'does not typically investigate sightings of unexplained phenomena outside the context of investigating potential threats or distress.' The Coast Guard has a procedure, Vital Intelligence Sightings handled as MERINT reports, and used it twice between 2010 and 2022.

The report is also candid about why the scientific community stays away. It cites a survey of 1,460 tenured and tenure-track faculty at 144 research-intensive U.S. universities in which 53 percent believed UAP-related research would jeopardize their tenure or promotion, while only 7 percent held a negative opinion of such research. That gap is the mechanics of stigma quantified, and it is the same mechanism ODNI described in 2021.

Into the gap stepped civilians. Chris Rutkowski, co-founder of Ufology Research, began investigating in 1975 and has published the annual Canadian UFO Survey since 1989. From 2000 he began receiving reports directly from federal agencies including DND and Transport Canada. By 2020, the report records, 'the direct submission of UFO reports from federal departments decreased significantly,' and Ufology Research now relies primarily on public sources and access-to-information requests. A federal-to-civilian data channel that operated for twenty years quietly closed at the exact moment the topic reopened in Washington.

The fourteen recommendations are structured to rebuild the capacity Canada dismantled. Identify a federal lead, one that is 'a trusted and recognized scientific organization' with public communication capacity and an established international network: 'For example, the Canadian Space Agency could be considered for such a role.' Establish a dedicated service that collects testimonies, investigates cases and posts analyses publicly. Have Transport Canada encourage pilots, cabin crews and air traffic controllers to report without fear of stigmatization. Support public dialogue, mandate intra-governmental collaboration by internal directive, improve media relations, convene an expert panel on countering misinformation, facilitate open data, conduct periodic surveys, and support citizen science. And, internationally, 'establish partnerships with international entities dedicated to UAPs, such as AARO and NASA (U.S.A.), GEIPAN (France), and SEFAA (Chile).'

That last recommendation is where the board should slow down. The report holds up AARO, established in 2022, as a structured, transparent model, alongside NASA's September 2023 study, France's GEIPAN inside CNES since 1977, and Chile's SEFAA. Canada is being advised to plug into the American architecture at the moment that architecture is under sustained challenge from congressional witnesses who characterize AARO as a barrier rather than a bridge. If Canada builds a UAP office whose primary international relationship is with AARO, then whatever AARO does not share, Canada will not have, and Canadian access-to-information law will not reach it. During the project the OCSA received about 250 inquiries from Canadians, including requests for help interpreting photos and videos, despite the website stating it offered no such service. That is an unanswered demand signal no Canadian institution is positioned to meet.

### What it has denied

Canada's operative denial is structural rather than rhetorical. The 1952-1954 Project Second Storey committee concluded that UFOs 'neither posed a security threat, nor were of scientific interest' and recommended ending all military investigation, and that finding has never been formally revisited. Present-day departments do not deny the phenomenon; they decline to record it, with the RCMP unable to produce any statistics at all and Transport Canada explicitly not investigating absent a serious safety concern.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, oversight, concealment, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Has the Government of Canada acted on any of the fourteen recommendations, and has a lead department been designated?
- Why did direct submission of UAP reports from DND and Transport Canada to Ufology Research decrease significantly around 2020, was that a policy decision, and by whom?
- What is in the roughly 5,500 pages of the Library and Archives Canada UFO collection that have not been digitized?
- Does the Department of National Defence hold UAP-related operational reporting outside the public record, and was DND's holding examined or merely consulted during the Sky Canada Project?
- Was the Project Second Storey finding of 1954 ever formally reviewed or rescinded, or does it remain the operative Canadian military position by default?
- What did the OCSA's consultations with DND and the security services produce that did not make it into the published 59-page report?
- How many of the 250 public inquiries to the OCSA involved imagery or physical evidence, and where was that material directed?
- Does NORAD's binational structure mean Canadian UAP radar data is held on the U.S. side and therefore outside Canadian access-to-information reach?
- Did Wilbert Smith's Project Magnet correspondence with U.S. counterparts survive in the LAC collection, and does it corroborate his claims about a classified American study?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 554 of 1111, score 29). 8 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D3.

## 'The Pentagon's UFO Program Is Real [Here's How It Works!]'

'The Pentagon's UFO Program Is Real [Here's How It Works!]' is a document on the board; board tier C, contextual, real and relevant, no direct programme linkage on the record yet; dates 2026; axes C0 A0 D3; 7 connections; in the research band.

In one line: American Alchemy episode, 11 Jan 2026.

### What it is

The long-form interview 'The Hidden UFO Program Is Real - Here's How It Works! (ft. UAPGerb),' published 11 January 2026 on American Alchemy with Jesse Michels, running three hours and fifty-four minutes. In it the pseudonymous open-source researcher UAPGerb walks Michels through a claimed organizational map of the legacy recovery and reverse-engineering effort: the contractor hierarchy, the administering agencies, the facilities, and the named individuals. It is the most detailed publicly available attempt to render the concealment structure as an org chart rather than an anecdote.

### Why it is implicated

This episode matters not as testimony but as a hypothesis document. Its published chapter structure is itself a claim about how the program is organized, 'Legacy Programs,' 'The Pyramid of Contractors,' 'Modern Majestic 12,' 'The Administrators of Secrecy,' 'The Office of Global Access,' 'The Role of MITRE,' 'The DOE NEST,' 'The 1954 Atomic Energy Agreement,' 'The Y-12 Complex,' 'Underground Military Bases', asserting that the effort is administered through a layered contractor pyramid with Department of Energy and federally funded research and development center involvement rather than through a single DoD program office. That is a falsifiable structural model, and it is the one an analyst can test against procurement records, facility clearances and statutory authorities. The episode also names individuals and cases as nodes, Jonathan Wagan, J. Andrew Kistner, Eric Henry Wang, Marconi, converting rumor into a checkable list. Its evidentiary weight is low; its investigative value as a target list is high.

### The longer account

There is a difference between a witness and a cartographer. Most UAP media is witness content: a person describes what he saw or was told, and the audience is asked to weigh his credibility. The Michels-Gerb episode is cartography. UAPGerb is not claiming personal access; he is claiming that the public record, read carefully enough and cross-indexed hard enough, discloses the shape of the thing. That makes the episode assessable in a way testimony is not, because a map can be checked against the terrain.

The episode published on 11 January 2026 at 7:15 PM UTC on Jesse Michels's American Alchemy, running 3 hours 54 minutes. The chapter list published with it is the closest thing the open record has to a table of contents for the concealment hypothesis, and it is worth reading as a document in itself.

The first movement is structural. 'Legacy Programs' at 6:00, then 'The Pyramid of Contractors' at 12:03, then 'Whistleblowers and Leaks' at 16:46 and 'Modern Majestic 12' at 23:50. The claim embedded in that ordering is that the program's continuity lives in the contractor layer rather than in any government office, that government offices rotate, get renamed, get terminated by statute, while the corporate custodians and their facility clearances persist. This is the single most consequential structural assertion in the episode, because it is the one that explains how a program survives the abolition of the Aerial Phenomena Task Force, the creation of AARO, and forty years of reorganizations. It is also the assertion that makes the 'covered government contractors' clause of the Burlison FY2027 amendment the operative provision rather than a throwaway.

The second movement is administrative. 'Exploring the IPU Docs' at 27:10 reaches back to the 1947 Interplanetary Phenomenon Unit; 'The Administrators of Secrecy' at 32:24 and 'Individuals in the Shadows' at 37:38 name people; 'The Office of Global Access' at 43:04 and 'The Conflict of Interest' at 49:48 lead into 'The Role of MITRE' at 51:28. MITRE operates federally funded research and development centers, organizations that hold government facility clearances and perform government work while sitting outside both the civil service and ordinary contractor competition. In the legacy-program hypothesis the FFRDC is the ideal custodian: it has continuity, clearances, and a relationship to government that is neither employment nor procurement in the ordinary sense.

The third movement is nuclear. 'The DOE NEST' at 1:22:01, 'The 1954 Atomic Energy Agreement' at 1:30:25, 'The GAO Audit' at 1:31:55, 'The Y-12 Complex' at 1:32:56. The Department of Energy thread is analytically the strongest part of the map, for a reason that has nothing to do with anyone's testimony: Restricted Data under the Atomic Energy Act of 1954 is classified by statute at birth, sits outside the Executive Order 13526 classification system entirely, and is therefore outside the reach of the Information Security Oversight Office's oversight authority. Congress noticed this. When it drafted the UAP authorized-disclosure protections at 50 U.S.C. 3373b(b)(1)(B), it wrote that an authorized disclosure 'shall be deemed to comply with any regulation or order issued under the authority of Executive Order 13526... or chapter 18 of the Atomic Energy Act of 1954.' Legislators do not immunize disclosures against the Atomic Energy Act unless they have reason to think the Atomic Energy Act is being used.

The fourth movement is geographic and biographical: 'Underground Military Bases' at 1:44:25, 'Covert Operations and Facilities' at 1:52:44, 'Marconi's Involvement' at 2:04:48, 'The Legacy of Eric Henry Wang' at 2:07:16, and named witness segments for Jonathan Wagan at 1:15:47 and J. Andrew Kistner at 1:31:35. The fifth is meta: 'Controlled Disclosure Dynamics' at 2:28:42, 'The Shadow Government' at 2:38:42, 'The Role of Psyops' at 2:51:34, Gerb arguing that disclosure itself is being managed, which is a claim about the current period rather than the historical one.

On sourcing discipline, this dossier draws a hard line. The episode's existence, date, duration, host, guest and published chapter structure are verifiable primary metadata. Every substantive assertion inside it, that MITRE holds a custodial role, that an 'Office of Global Access' exists, that Y-12 is implicated, that a 1954 atomic energy agreement bears on the subject, is UAPGerb's claim, unverified here, and is catalogued as such at tier 3. The correct use of this episode by a downstream analyst is as a query list: take each named entity, each facility, each individual, and test it against procurement data, FOIA releases, clearance records and statutory authority. The episode's value is that it is specific enough to be wrong.

The relationship to the broader board is that Gerb's structural model and Congress's legislative behaviour have converged without either citing the other. Gerb says the continuity lives in the contractor pyramid. Congress, in the FY2023 authorized-disclosure statute and again in the FY2027 House-adopted disclosure amendment, keeps writing provisions that reach contractors, contractor nondisclosure agreements, and contractor-held records. Two independent processes arriving at the same load-bearing assumption is the kind of convergence worth flagging.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Every substantive assertion made inside the episode is unverified here. The chapter structure is primary metadata; the content is UAPGerb's claim.
- No documentary basis has been established for the existence of an 'Office of Global Access,' and no government issuance, budget line or organizational chart naming such an office has been identified.
- MITRE's asserted role rests on no cited contract, task order or FFRDC sponsoring agreement in the public record examined here.
- The identities, employment histories and clearance records of Jonathan Wagan, J. Andrew Kistner and Eric Henry Wang have not been independently established.
- What 'The 1954 Atomic Energy Agreement' refers to specifically, whether the Atomic Energy Act of 1954 itself, an implementing agreement, or a separate instrument, is not resolved by the chapter title.
- What 'The GAO Audit' segment refers to; the Comptroller General does hold a UAP audit mandate under Section 6803 of Public Law 117-263, but whether that is the audit discussed is not established.
- Whether UAPGerb's identity, background and any prior government or contractor affiliation would change the weight assigned to his structural model.
- Whether any of the facilities or organizations named in the episode have been the subject of a UAP-related records search under 50 U.S.C. 3373b(b)(3)(A).

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded C on a global pass (rank 764 of 1111, score 22). 7 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D3.

## NASA UAP Independent Study Team

NASA UAP Independent Study Team is a program on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2022 to 2023; axes C0 A1 D3; 7 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Federal advisory study team, 2022-2023.

### What it is

The NASA Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Independent Study Team was a 16-member external panel convened by NASA in 2022 under the Earth Science Advisory Committee, chaired by astrophysicist David Spergel of the Simons Foundation, with Dr. Daniel Evans of NASA Headquarters as Designated Federal Official. It held a single public meeting on 31 May 2023 and delivered its final report on 14 September 2023. On the same day NASA created a Director of UAP Research position and initially declined to name the appointee.

### Why it is implicated

The panel is a study in the shape of a hole. It was chartered to advise NASA on UAP using only unclassified civilian, commercial and open-source data, and it says so plainly, which means the one body assembled to bring scientific rigor to the question was structurally barred from the classified holdings where the question actually lives. Its report says the quiet part in print: military and intelligence sensor data are classified 'often because of what the imagery could reveal about U.S. technical capabilities to our adversaries, and not because of what is actually in the images,' and that these classified observations 'enhance the sense of mystery and conspiracy surrounding UAP, and they present an obstacle to scientific inquiry.' That is a federally chartered panel, in an official NASA publication, stating that classification is generating the appearance of concealment. Three of its sixteen members came from commercial remote-sensing, space-hardware and private-equity defense firms, Maxar, Redwire and AE Industrial Partners, and two from the FAA, making the panel a map of who holds the civilian sensor data that would matter. And the harassment record around the panel and the withheld name of NASA's UAP research director are themselves artifacts: the cost of association was high enough that the agency initially treated the identity of a civil servant as a security matter.

### The longer account

NASA's Independent Study Team was born of a specific institutional bet: that the agency with the most public trust and the least security baggage could drag UAP into normal science. The bet was placed by Administrator Bill Nelson and executed by the Science Mission Directorate under Nicola Fox, following her predecessor Thomas Zurbuchen, with the activity graciously hosted, in the report's own words, by NASA's Earth Science Division under the Earth Science Advisory Committee. That administrative placement is worth pausing on: a panel about unidentified objects in the airspace was parked in the division that studies the planet's surface and atmosphere. It kept the activity out of astrophysics and out of anything that would have implied a search for technology.

The roster reads as a deliberate coalition of civilian instrumentation. Chair David Spergel of the Simons Foundation. Anamaria Berea of George Mason, Federica Bianco of Delaware, Paula Bontempi of Rhode Island, Jennifer Buss of the Potomac Institute for Policy Studies, science journalist Nadia Drake, David Grinspoon of the Planetary Science Institute, retired astronaut Captain Scott Kelly, Matt Mountain of AURA, Joshua Semeter of Boston University, and Shelley Wright of UC San Diego. Then the operators: Warren Randolph and Karlin Toner from the Federal Aviation Administration, Walter Scott from Maxar Technologies, Mike Gold from Redwire Space, and Reggie Brothers from AE Industrial Partners. Two FAA officials, one commercial satellite imaging executive, one space infrastructure executive, and one defense-focused private equity operator who had previously been Under Secretary for Science and Technology at DHS. If the panel's recommendations pointed anywhere, they pointed at data these institutions own.

And they did. The report recommends NASA leverage its Earth-observing assets to probe local environmental conditions associated with UAP detected by other means, and 'explore enhancing collaborations with the U.S. commercial remote-sensing industry, which offer powerful constellations of high-resolution Earth-observing satellites.' It recommends multispectral and hyperspectral acquisition, AI and machine learning applied to anomaly detection, a crowdsourcing system built on open-source smartphone apps, and better leveraging of the Aviation Safety Reporting System, which NASA operates for the FAA, as 'a critical database for the whole-of-government effort.' Every one of those recommendations routes future UAP data through civilian and commercial channels rather than military ones. Read charitably, that is a strategy for building an evidentiary base outside the classification perimeter. Read uncharitably, it is a strategy for building an evidentiary base that will never intersect the classification perimeter.

The panel's own frustration surfaces in the passage that matters most. Discussing why existing observations cannot resolve UAP, the report states: 'In addition, much of the data collected by military sensors or intelligence satellites are classified, often because of what the imagery could reveal about U.S. technical capabilities to our adversaries, and not because of what is actually in the images. While essential for security, these classified observations enhance the sense of mystery and conspiracy surrounding UAP, and they present an obstacle to scientific inquiry.' A federal advisory panel wrote, in a public report, that the government's classification practice is manufacturing the appearance of a cover-up and blocking the science that would dispel it. That sentence is a finding about the concealment architecture, delivered by scientists who could not see inside it.

On the evidence question the report is careful and narrow: 'To date, in the peer-reviewed scientific literature, there is no conclusive evidence suggesting an extraterrestrial origin for UAP.' The qualifier is doing work, peer-reviewed literature is precisely the corpus from which classified holdings are absent. The panel also recorded that then-AARO Director Sean Kirkpatrick reported at its 31 May 2023 public meeting that AARO had collected more than 800 reported events, against 263 reports filed in the 17 years before March 2021.

The cost of participation is documented in the report itself: team members received hate mail from colleagues, were ridiculed and criticized on social media, and had firsthand knowledge of colleagues warned away from technosignature research on the grounds that it could damage scientific credibility and promotion prospects. On 14 September 2023 NASA announced the creation of a Director of UAP Research and Administrator Nelson said the identity would be withheld to prevent harassment. NASA reversed within hours and named Mark McInerney, a former NASA liaison to the Department of Defense on UAP matters. The reversal is instructive twice over: it shows the agency's first instinct was concealment framed as protection, and it shows how quickly that instinct collapsed under press pressure, which is not how the Department of Defense's instances of the same instinct have behaved.

The statutory tie is direct. 50 U.S.C. 3373(c)(6) requires AARO to coordinate with NASA, among other agencies. Burlison's 22 May 2026 MITRE interrogatories demand identification of all UAP-related work MITRE performed for or with NASA. NASA sits inside the legal architecture of the program question while its own expert panel sat outside the data.

### What it has denied

NASA's posture is not denial but bounded agnosticism: the report asserts only that the peer-reviewed literature contains no conclusive evidence of extraterrestrial origin, while simultaneously stating that the relevant military and intelligence data are classified and inaccessible. NASA's initial decision to withhold the name of its own UAP research director, reversed within hours, is a concealment artifact in its own right.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- Did any panel member hold clearances permitting review of AARO or SAP-controlled material, or was the entire panel unclassified? The report describes unclassified sourcing but does not state member clearance status.
- Who selected the sixteen members, and against what criteria did three commercial space and defense-investment executives and two FAA officials join a scientific advisory panel?
- What did NASA's Terms of Reference, the eight charge elements the report responds to, actually say? The full Terms of Reference text is not reproduced in the public report summary.
- Which colleagues warned other scientists away from technosignature research, and were any of those warnings institutional rather than personal?
- Has NASA implemented any of the panel's recommendations, commercial remote-sensing collaboration, crowdsourced smartphone data, ASRS integration, and with what budget line?
- What UAP-related work, if any, has MITRE or another FFRDC performed for NASA? Burlison asked; no answer is public.
- Who made the initial decision to withhold the UAP research director's name, and on what threat assessment?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 371 of 1111, score 35). 8 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A1/D3.

- **Tier 1 documented.** The report records that Dr. Sean Kirkpatrick reported at the panel's public meeting that AARO had collected more than 800 reported events, including FAA data, against 263 reports filed in the 17 years prior to March 2021. - **Tier 1 documented.** The report states that much of the data collected by military sensors or intelligence satellites is classified 'often because of what the imagery could reveal about U.S. technical capabilities to our adversaries, and not because of what is actually in the images,' and that 'these classified observations enhance the sense of mystery and conspiracy surrounding UAP, and they present an obstacle to scientific inquiry.' Source: NASA UAP Independent Study Team Report, 14 September 2023, https://smd-cms.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/09/uap-independent-study-team-final-report.pdf

## AARO Historical Record Report, Volume II (unpublished)

AARO Historical Record Report, Volume II (unpublished) is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2024; axes C1 A2 D3; 6 connections; in the research band.

In one line: report.

### What it is

Volume II of the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office's Report on the Historical Record of U.S. Government Involvement with Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena, the sequel that Volume I (cleared for public release March 2024) explicitly promised. As of August 2026 no Volume II has been published, and AARO has also not issued its 2025 annual report. The entity is therefore a documented promise and a documented non-delivery rather than a document.

### Why it is implicated

Volume I did not close its own investigation; it closed the public phase of it. AARO named a Department of Homeland Security prospective special access program (KONA BLUE), disclosed that an Intelligence Community Controlled Access Program had its scope expanded in 2021 "to protect UAP reverse-engineering" and was then disestablished, and asserted that every alleged reverse-engineering program it examined either did not exist, was a misidentified authentic sensitive program, or resolved to "an unwarranted and disestablished program." Each of those three outcomes is an admission that named, real, compartmented activity exists behind the denial. Volume I then deferred everything unresolved to a Volume II that never came. The non-publication is the finding: the office that was granted, by its own account, "full, unrestricted access by all organizations" produced one volume of conclusions and no volume of the remaining work. A promised second volume that never appears converts a partial exculpation into a permanent one, because there is no dated deliverable against which anyone can measure what was left out.

### The longer account

The statutory hook is Section 6802 of the James M. Inhofe National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2023 (Public Law 117-263, enacted 23 December 2022), which rewrote the 2021 UAP framework, codified much of it at 50 U.S.C. 3373, and directed AARO to conduct a historical review of all U.S. Government UAP activity from 1 January 1945 forward, reporting no later than 540 days after enactment, roughly 15 June 2024. The statute reached the exact material a legacy program would generate: any program or activity protected by restricted access that had not been explicitly and clearly reported to Congress, and any efforts to obfuscate, manipulate public opinion, hide, or otherwise provide incorrect information about UAP. Congress also directed the Comptroller General to monitor the review's progress and brief designated members.

AARO answered in March 2024 with a document titled Volume I. The title itself is the tell. A single report was due; AARO delivered a first installment, front-loaded the conclusions, and moved the unfinished work into a second installment. Volume I states that AARO "remains open to additional, verifiable information that may alter the conclusions rendered in Volume I and will note any changes to the findings in this report in Volume II," and elsewhere commits to publishing Volume II in accordance with the Section 6802 date while confining it to information acquired after Volume I's publication. That construction is load-bearing: the statutory deadline is satisfied by Volume I, and Volume II inherits no independent enforceable date of its own. Nothing in the record obliges anyone to produce it, and nobody has.

What Volume I left on the table is specific. AARO disclosed KONA BLUE, a UAP-related Prospective Special Access Program pitched to DHS and "supported by individuals who believed the USG was hiding off-world technology," which DHS never approved. It disclosed that in 2021, "without sufficient justification, the scope of an IC Controlled Access Program was expanded to protect UAP reverse-engineering," and that the program "was disestablished due to its lack of merit." AARO does not name the IC program, does not name the original classification authority that expanded it, does not say who requested the expansion, and does not say what the program's pre-2021 scope was. It disclosed a materials analysis resolving a sample to magnesium "and bismuth with some other trace elements, such as lead," based on materials characterization. It attributed the reverse-engineering allegations to "circular reporting from a group of individuals" who "have been involved in various UAP-related endeavors since at least 2009," and tied many of them to "a cancelled DIA program and the subsequent but failed attempt to reestablish this program under DHS, called KONA BLUE", an unmistakable reference to AAWSAP/BAASS and the Bigelow-Puthoff-Davis network, named by function rather than by name.

Every one of those threads is a live investigative lead, and each was left dangling. Who inside the IC expanded a Controlled Access Program to cover reverse-engineering in 2021, and on what asserted basis, is exactly the question Congress wrote Section 6802 to answer. AARO's own framing concedes that at least one compartment did, for some period, formally exist to protect UAP reverse-engineering. Whether it protected a real capability or a phantom, someone signed the security classification guide.

The delivery record since then is thin. DefenseScoop reported on 25 February 2026 that AARO had published neither its 2025 annual report nor a second volume of the historical review, while its caseload exceeded 2,000 cases. The FY2026 NDAA (P.L. 119-60, signed 18 December 2025) added three new AARO obligations, NORTHCOM/NORAD intercept briefings, an accounting of UAP-related security classification guides, and consolidation of duplicative reporting, but did not compel Volume II. Congress layered new demands on top of an unfulfilled old one without noticing, or without minding, that the old one had lapsed.

Read as an artifact of the concealment effort, Volume I is an unusually well-executed document. It is signed, dated, sourced, and comprehensive in tone; it names two compartmented programs, concedes access to a third, and asserts that AARO went everywhere and found nothing. Its most consequential feature is structural rather than substantive: by splitting the report, AARO produced an authoritative-sounding public verdict while retaining the ability never to publish the working papers behind it. Two and a half years on, that is precisely what has happened.

### What it has denied

AARO's public position, first stated by Pentagon spokesperson Sue Gough within hours of the 5 June 2023 Grusch reporting, is that "AARO has not discovered any verifiable information to substantiate claims that any programs regarding the possession or reverse-engineering of extraterrestrial materials have existed in the past or exist currently." Volume I hardened that into a signed institutional finding and then deferred the unfinished portion of the inquiry to a volume that has never appeared. The denial is dated, attributable, and structurally unfalsifiable, because the working record supporting it was reserved for a sequel.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, oversight, disclosure, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Which IC element sponsored the Controlled Access Program whose scope was expanded in 2021 to protect UAP reverse-engineering, who authorized the expansion, and what was the program's scope before 2021?
- Does any internal AARO deliverable, draft, or interim product labeled Volume II exist in any classification, and if so who holds it?
- Did AARO ever formally notify Congress that Volume II would not be produced, and did any committee ask?
- Did the Comptroller General's Section 6802 monitoring role produce written products, and have they been released?
- Volume I says AARO conducted approximately 30 interviews; who were they, and how many refused, invoked counsel, or were denied clearance to speak?
- Which specific 'authentic, highly sensitive national security programs' were misidentified by interviewees as reverse-engineering programs, AARO asserts they exist but names none.
- Who led AARO at the moment Volume I was cleared, and what internal dissent, if any, was recorded against its conclusions?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 46 of 1111, score 82). 9 connections, 3 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 3 official denials recorded against it. Axes C1/A2/D3.

The sequence is fixed by the document itself rather than inferred. L. 117-263) - AARO establishment, Historical Record Report and annual reports** (legislation, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: the same requirement, unmet. Volume II answers to the same subsection (j) obligation. The statute requires a report on the historical record from 1 January 1945 to the completion of the Director's activities; Volume I states on its own concluding page that Volume II 'will be published in accordance with the date es[tablished]'. Volume II is the outstanding half of a statutory requirement, which is why the absence is on the board as a node rather than as a gap. Sources: Pub. L. 117-263 (James M. ## Americans for Safe Aerospace

Americans for Safe Aerospace is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2022; axes C0 A0 D3; 6 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Aircrew advocacy and UAP reporting nonprofit.

### What it is

Americans for Safe Aerospace (ASA) is a military pilot-led 501(c)(3) nonprofit founded in 2023 by Ryan Graves, a former U.S. Navy F/A-18F pilot from strike fighter squadron VFA-11. It operates a confidential intake channel through which military and commercial aircrew report UAP encounters, and it functions as the principal non-governmental witness pipeline into Congress. Headquartered at 1620 I St NW Suite 900, Washington, DC, it describes itself as the world's largest UAP advocacy community and reported more than 30,000 members as of 2026, up from roughly 4,000 at its founding.

### Why it is implicated

ASA is the institution that solved the collection problem the government created. Because there is no protected, non-stigmatizing reporting route for commercial pilots, and because military witnesses face a confusing internal process, the observational data that would test the phenomenon has never been aggregated by the state. ASA aggregates it privately and then feeds it to Congress, which is why its founder's July 2023 testimony landed as the first public congressional hearing to take military pilot and whistleblower evidence. More importantly for a concealment map, ASA is where several people who were inside the classified system have landed: Jay Stratton, former Director of the UAP Task Force; Christopher Mellon, former Deputy Assistant Secretary of Defense for Intelligence; Rear Admiral Tim Gallaudet, former head of NOAA and Oceanographer of the Navy; and Kirk McConnell, former professional staff of the Senate Armed Services Committee. That roster means ASA holds institutional memory of both the task force that collected the data and the committee staff that wrote the oversight statutes. It is a private organization staffed with the government's own former custodians.

### The longer account

ASA's origin is a specific failure of the reporting system. Graves joined the Navy in 2009 and by 2014 was flying with VFA-11 near Virginia Beach when a radar upgrade began producing unknown tracks that were initially dismissed as software glitches and then corroborated on infrared, confirming their physical presence. The sightings became routine enough to appear in daily briefs. The pivotal event was in Warning Area W-72, an exclusive block of airspace ten miles east of Virginia Beach where all traffic enters through a single GPS point at a set altitude. As two jets crossed the threshold one hundred feet apart, one pilot saw a dark gray cube inside a clear sphere, motionless against the wind, fixed directly at the entry point. Both aircraft took evasive action and terminated the mission. The squadron filed a safety report. There was no official acknowledgement and no mechanism to report further sightings. Graves founded ASA to build the mechanism that did not exist.

The organization scaled faster than anyone planned. Its July 18, 2023 launch release for the UAP Witness Campaign stated that more than 30 commercial aircrew and military witnesses had already approached it and that ASA had more than 4,000 members. Eight days later, in written testimony to the House Oversight Subcommittee on National Security, the Border, and Foreign Affairs, Graves reported nearly 5,000 members and more than 30 UAP witnesses being actively worked. By 2026 the public membership figure is over 30,000. The witness profile is itself a finding: the majority are commercial pilots at major airlines, often veterans with decades of experience, and their reports frequently open with an apology, in Graves's phrasing, 'I apologize, I realize this will sound crazy.' They describe objects at altitudes apparently above 40,000 feet, potentially in low earth orbit or in the grey zone below the Karman line, executing right-hand turns, retrograde orbits and j-hooks, with recurring clusters north of Hawaii and in the North Atlantic. They are adamant these are not Starlink flares.

ASA's process is deliberately witness-controlled. A pilot or veteran contacts the organization confidentially with an outline, ASA conducts an interview, brings in an advisor with relevant expertise for evaluation, and only then, and only with the witness's explicit permission, are next steps taken, which can include meetings with congressional staff and Members or with AARO. Most witnesses do not want to speak publicly and fear professional consequences; they want their account added to the data set. That design is a direct response to the retaliation problem that runs through the whole file.

The advisory bench is what makes ASA structurally significant rather than merely large. Its pilots include David Fravor and Alex Dietrich, the first former Navy pilots to meet with Congress about their UAP encounter, plus Michael Greene and Sam Fletcher. Its national security advisors are Jay Stratton, former Director of the UAP Task Force, Christopher Mellon, Rear Admiral Tim Gallaudet, and Kirk McConnell, former professional staff on the Senate Armed Services Committee, which is the committee that wrote the AARO statutes and whose chair and ranking member sit inside the twelve-member waived-SAP notification group. Its policy advisors include Susan McCue, former chief of staff to Senator Harry Reid, and Bryan Bender, former defense editor at Politico, plus Mike Gold of the NASA UAP Independent Study Team and David Radzanowski, former CFO and Chief of Staff at NASA. Its science bench includes Avi Loeb of Harvard, Garry Nolan of Stanford, Robin Hanson of George Mason and Ravi Starzl of Carnegie Mellon.

The single most operationally important thing ASA has put on the congressional record came through Gallaudet. Graves testified that while serving as an Admiral with Fleet Forces Command, Gallaudet received a classified SIPRNET email in 2015 from the Operations Commander to all one- and two-star subordinates, titled 'Urgent safety of flight issue', attaching the GO FAST video and raising concerns about multiple near mid-air collisions with UAP in the early warning area off Virginia Beach, with a note that the exercise might be shut down for safety reasons. Gallaudet reviewed it with his deputy. The next day the email was removed from both of their systems, and despite routine in-person meetings with that group, no one ever discussed it again. Gallaudet's stated presumption is that the removal was connected to a classified special access program. That is a flag officer, on the record through a congressional witness, describing the retroactive deletion of a safety-of-flight communication.

Graves also entered two hard data points into the House record: that the UAP Task Force reported in 2021 there had been eleven near misses with UAP, citing the ODNI Preliminary Assessment of June 25, 2021, and that in April 2022 the FAA issued an alert to its operations managers that a commercial aircraft over West Virginia experienced a double attitude and double autopilot failure while flying under a UAP. He also flagged an unexplained reversal by AARO's director, who told Congress he operated only under Title 10 authority and needed Title 50 authorities and access to scientifically calibrated intelligence-community sensors, then indicated in his first public interview shortly before the hearing that he had what he needed.

On policy, ASA endorsed the Safe Airspace for Americans Act, H.R. 6967 in the 118th Congress, which would let civilian pilots report through NASA's Aviation Safety Reporting System with anti-retaliation protection and public release of all reports, a mechanism recommended by the NASA UAP Independent Study Team. ASA's July 2023 release also documents the organization's read of the UAP Disclosure Act of 2023, sponsored by Senators Schumer, Rounds, Rubio, Gillibrand, Young and Heinrich, whose central mechanism was a nine-member civilian Review Board empowered to hold hearings, administer oaths, and subpoena UAP witnesses and documents.

### What it has denied

ASA does not deny; it documents. Its posture toward the government's own position is captured in Graves's testimony noting that AARO director Sean Kirkpatrick told Congress he operated only under Title 10 authority and needed Title 50 authorities and access to scientifically calibrated intelligence-community sensors, then indicated in his first public interview a week before the hearing that he had what he needed, a reversal Graves flagged on the record as unexplained.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, research, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- How many of ASA's accumulated witness reports have actually been transmitted to AARO, and what AARO did with them
- Whether ASA's raw report corpus is preserved, structured, and available to any independent analytic body
- Whether any ASA witness has faced documented retaliation after referral
- The current disposition of the April 2022 FAA West Virginia double-failure alert, which has not been publicly produced in full
- Whether ASA advisors who held clearances are constrained by nondisclosure obligations that the July 2026 presidential UAP NDA directive may now have waived
- Whether ASA's 30,000-plus membership figure reflects verified aviation professionals or general public sign-ups; the site does not disaggregate

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 475 of 1111, score 31). 6 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A0/D3.
